\check{e}_{i}^{1}\wedge\overline{\check{e}}_{i}^{1}=\sum_{l,j=2}^{n_{p}}\alpha_{li}\overline{\alpha_{ji}}dz^{l}\wedge d\bar{z}^{j}+\text{terms with }dz^{1}\text{ or }d\bar{z}^{1}.

Thus by (
6.34
), (
6.36
), (
6.35
),
and (
3.4
),
\langle\frac{\rho_{1}^{l}}{l!},\frac{\chi_{\mathcal{B}}^{l}}{l!}\rangle=\sigma_{l}(\tilde{A}_{1}^{-1})

and similarly

(6.37)

\langle\frac{\rho_{i}^{l}}{l!},\frac{\chi_{\mathcal{B}}^{l}}{l!}\rangle=\sigma_{l}(\tilde{A}_{i}^{-1}).

Let

(6.38)

\tilde{\xi}=\langle\check{e}_{1}^{1},\xi\rangle e_{1}^{1}+\sum_{i=n_{p}+1}^{n_{p}+d_{1}-1}\xi^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{i}}.

Then, by (
6.5
), we have

(6.39)

\displaystyle\langle\frac{\rho_{1}^{k_{1}}}{k_{1}!},2\sqrt{-1}\xi\wedge\bar{\xi}\wedge\exp\chi_{\mathcal{B}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle T_{k_{1}-1}(\tilde{A}_{1}^{-1}),\tilde{\xi}^{\dagger}\tilde{\xi}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\sigma_{k_{1}-1}(\tilde{A}_{1}^{-1})}{\sigma_{d_{1}-1}(\tilde{A}_{1}^{-1})}\langle T_{d_{1}-1}(\tilde{A}_{1}^{-1}),\tilde{\xi}^{\dagger}\tilde{\xi}\rangle.

Notice that

(6.40)

T_{d_{1}-1}(\tilde{A}_{1}^{-1})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\sigma_{d_{1}-1}(\tilde{A}_{1}^{-1})&0\\
0&T^{\prime}\end{array}\right),

where
T^{\prime}\geq 0
is a non-negative Hermitian matrix. Thus, from (
6.39
)
and (
6.40
), we have

(6.41)

\displaystyle\langle\frac{\rho_{1}^{k_{1}}}{k_{1}!},2\sqrt{-1}\xi\wedge\bar{\xi}\wedge\exp\chi_{\mathcal{B}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\geq\frac{\sigma_{k_{1}-1}(\tilde{A}_{1}^{-1})}{\sigma_{d_{1}-1}(\tilde{A}_{1}^{-1})}\sigma_{d_{1}-1}(\tilde{A}_{1}^{-1})|\langle\check{e}_{1}^{1},\xi\rangle|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{k_{1}-1}(\tilde{A}_{1}^{-1})|\langle\check{e}_{1}^{1},\xi\rangle|^{2}.

Apply the same argument on each
\mathcal{V}_{i}
to obtain

(6.42)

\langle\Lambda^{\prime},2\sqrt{-1}\xi\wedge\bar{\xi}\wedge\exp\chi_{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n_{p}}\sigma_{k_{i}-1}(\tilde{A}_{i}^{-1})|\langle\check{e}_{1}^{i},\xi\rangle|^{2},

where
\Lambda^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{n_{p}}(\exp\rho_{i})^{[k_{i}]}.
On the
other hand, by applying Lemma
6.3
(2), we have

(6.43)

\sigma_{n-1}\left((A|1)^{-1}\right)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n_{p}}\sigma_{d_{i}-1}(\tilde{A}_{1}^{-1})\prod_{j\not=i}\sigma_{d_{j}}(\tilde{A}_{j}^{-1}).

Let
s_{i}=\sigma_{k_{i}}(\tilde{A}_{i}^{-1})^{\frac{1}{k_{i}}}
.
Then by Newton-Maclaurin inequality,

(6.44)

\sigma_{k_{i}-1}(\tilde{A}_{i}^{-1})\geq\binom{d_{i}}{k_{i}-1}\binom{d_{i}}{k_{i}}^{\frac{1}{k_{i}}-1}s_{i}^{k_{i}-1},

(6.45)

\sum_{i=1}^{n_{p}}\sigma_{d_{i}-1}(\tilde{A}_{i}^{-1})\prod_{j\not=i}\sigma_{d_{j}}(\tilde{A}_{j}^{-1})\leq\frac{\max_{i}\{d_{i}\binom{d_{i}}{k_{i}}^{\frac{1}{k_{i}}}\}}{\prod_{i=1}^{n_{p}}\binom{d_{i}}{k_{i}}^{\frac{d_{i}}{k_{i}}}}\prod_{i=1}^{n_{p}}s_{i}^{d_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{p}}s_{j}^{-1}.

Thus, by (
6.42
), (
6.43
), (
6.44
),
and (
6.45
), we get

(6.46)

\displaystyle\langle\Lambda^{\prime},2\sqrt{-1}\xi\wedge\bar{\xi}\wedge\exp\chi_{\mathcal{B}}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq\sigma_{n-1}((A|1))^{-1}\cdot\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n_{p}}\sigma_{k_{i}-1}(\tilde{A}_{i}^{-1})|\langle\check{e}_{1}^{i},\xi\rangle|^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n_{p}}\sigma_{d_{i}-1}(\tilde{A}_{1}^{-1})\prod_{j\not=i}\sigma_{d_{j}}(\tilde{A}_{j}^{-1})}

\displaystyle=\det(A)^{-1}\hat{a}\cdot\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n_{p}}\sigma_{k_{i}-1}(\tilde{A}_{i}^{-1})|\langle\check{e}_{1}^{i},\xi\rangle|^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n_{p}}\sigma_{d_{i}-1}(\tilde{A}_{1}^{-1})\prod_{j\not=i}\sigma_{d_{j}}(\tilde{A}_{j}^{-1})}

\displaystyle\geq c_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{p})\cdot\hat{a}\cdot\det(A)^{-1}\cdot\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n_{p}}s_{i}^{k_{i}-1}|\langle\check{e}_{1}^{i},\xi\rangle|^{2}}{\prod_{i=1}^{n_{p}}s_{i}^{d_{i}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{p}}s_{i}^{-1}},

where

(6.47)

c_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{p})=\frac{\min_{i}\left\{\binom{d_{i}}{k_{i}-1}\binom{d_{i}}{k_{i}}^{\frac{1}{k_{i}}-1}\right\}\prod_{i=1}^{n_{p}}\binom{d_{i}}{k_{i}}^{\frac{d_{i}}{k_{i}}}}{\max_{i}\{d_{i}\binom{d_{i}}{k_{i}}^{\frac{1}{k_{i}}}\}}.

On the other hand, by the cone condition and Lemma
6.4
,
we have
s_{i}\leq(\frac{\kappa}{m})^{\frac{1}{k_{i}}}
. Since
\langle\xi,b\rangle=1

and
b\in\text{span}\{\check{e}_{1}^{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{p}}
, we have

\sum_{i=1}^{n_{p}}|\langle\check{e}_{1}^{i},\xi\rangle|^{2}\geq 1
. Define
a function

(6.48)

\gamma(s_{1},\cdots,s_{n_{p}}):=c_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{p})\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n_{p}}s_{i}^{k_{i}-1}|\langle\check{e}_{1}^{i},\xi\rangle|^{2}}{\prod_{i=1}^{n_{p}}s_{i}^{d_{i}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{p}}s_{i}^{-1}}.

Then
\gamma
is decreasing in each
s_{i}
. Since
s_{i}\leq(\frac{\kappa}{m})^{\frac{1}{k_{i}}}
,
we have

(6.49)

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle\geq c_{0}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n_{p}}\left(\frac{\kappa}{m}\right)^{1-\frac{1}{k_{i}}}|\langle\check{e}_{1}^{i},\xi\rangle|^{2}}{(\frac{\kappa}{m})^{\sum_{i=1}^{n_{p}}\frac{d_{i}}{k_{i}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{p}}\left(\frac{\kappa}{m}\right)^{-\frac{1}{k_{i}}}}

\displaystyle\geq c_{0}\frac{\min\{\left(\frac{\kappa}{m}\right)^{1-\frac{1}{k_{i}}}\}}{(\frac{\kappa}{m})^{\sum_{i=1}^{n_{p}}\frac{d_{i}}{k_{i}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{p}}\left(\frac{\kappa}{m}\right)^{-\frac{1}{k_{i}}}}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{\min}(\frac{\kappa}{m},n_{p},\mathbf{d}_{p},\mathbf{k}_{p}).

Thus, by (
6.46
) and (
6.49
), we have

(6.50)

\langle\Lambda^{\prime},2\sqrt{-1}\xi\wedge\bar{\xi}\wedge\exp\chi_{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\geq\hat{a}\cdot\det A^{-1}\cdot\gamma_{\min}.

Notice
m\Lambda^{\prime}\leq\mathring{\Lambda}
and
\hat{a}=\frac{1}{\|b\|_{\omega}^{2}}.

Hence, (
6.30
) follows from (
6.50
)
immediately.
∎

An immediate consequence of Lemma
6.5
is the
monotonicity of
F(A)
.

Lemma 6.6
.

Suppose
\mathring{\Lambda}
satisfies

\mathcal{O}
-UP and for all
p\in M
,
f(p)>-m\gamma_{\min}(\frac{\kappa}{m},n_{p},\mathbf{d}_{p},\mathbf{k}_{p})
.
If
\omega
satisfies the cone condition (
1.5
)
at a point
p
, then

(6.51)

-F^{i\bar{j}}(A)b_{i}\bar{b_{j}}\geq-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}F_{k}^{i\bar{j}}b_{i}\bar{b}_{j}>0,

for any non-zero covector
b=b_{i}dz^{i}
. As a result,
F(A)
is
strictly decreasing in
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}^{\kappa}
.

Proof.

From (
4.1
), we have

(6.52)

-F_{k}^{i\bar{j}}(A)b_{i}\bar{b}_{j}=\langle\Lambda^{[k]},2\sqrt{-1}b^{\sharp}\wedge\bar{b^{\sharp}}\wedge\exp\chi\rangle.

Let
\mathcal{B}=\{\xi\in\mathcal{T}_{p}M:b(\xi)=0\}
. Since
A^{-1}|_{\mathcal{B}}\geq\left(A|_{\mathcal{B}}\right)^{-1}

by Lemmas
3.5
and
3.2
,

\exp\chi\geq_{s}\exp\chi_{\mathcal{B}}
. Thus, if we take
\xi=b^{\sharp}

in (
6.30
), then we have

(6.53)

-\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}F_{k}^{i\bar{j}}b_{i}\bar{b}_{j}\geq\langle\Lambda^{[k]},2\sqrt{-1}b^{\sharp}\wedge\bar{b^{\sharp}}\wedge\exp\chi_{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\geq m\gamma_{\min}(\frac{\kappa}{m},n_{p},\mathbf{d}_{p},\mathbf{k}_{p})\frac{\|b\|_{\omega}^{2}}{\det A}.

Direct computation shows that

\displaystyle-F^{i\bar{j}}b_{i}\bar{b}_{j}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}F_{k}^{i\bar{j}}b_{i}\bar{b}_{j}+\frac{f(p)}{\det A}A^{\bar{j}i}b_{i}\overline{b_{j}}

Then, from Lemma
6.5
, we have

(6.54)

\displaystyle-F^{i\bar{j}}b_{i}\bar{b}_{j}

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}F_{k}^{i\bar{j}}b_{i}\bar{b}_{j}+\left(\frac{m}{2}\gamma_{\min}(\frac{\kappa}{m},n_{p},\mathbf{d}_{p},\mathbf{k}_{p})+f(p)\right)\frac{\|b\|_{\omega}^{2}}{\det A}

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}F_{k}^{i\bar{j}}b_{i}\bar{b}_{j}>0.

∎

Now we prove Lemma
6.1
.

Proof of Lemma
6.1
.

We consider a generic point
p_{1}\in\mathcal{M}
. If
f(p_{1})\geq 0
,
the cone condition holds automatically. Thus, we may assume
f(p_{1})<0.

Let
\gamma(s),
s\in[0,1]
be a curve connecting
p_{0}
and
p_{1}
.
Let
s_{0}=\min\{s\in(0,1],\ \omega(\gamma(s))\notin\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}^{\kappa}\}

and let
p=\gamma(s_{0})
. By Lemma
5.8
,
the degeneracy of cone condition implies that we can find a rank 1
Hermitian matrix
B=b^{\dagger}b
such that

(6.55)

F_{\Lambda}(A:B)=\mathcal{P}_{\Lambda}(A)=\kappa.

At
p
, we pick a normal coordinate of
\rho
as in Lemma
6.5
.
Let

(6.56)

\chi=2\sqrt{-1}\sum_{i,j}A^{\bar{j}i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}^{j}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{i}},\ \chi_{\infty}=2\sqrt{-1}\sum_{i,j>1}(A|1)^{\bar{j}i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}^{j}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{i}},

where
((A|1)^{\bar{j}i})
is the inverse matrix of
(A|1)
. From
(
6.55
),
\langle\Lambda,\exp(\chi_{\infty})\rangle=\kappa.

From equation (
1.2
),
\langle\Lambda,\exp(\chi)\rangle=\kappa.

Thus, we have

\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\frac{\langle\Lambda^{[k]},\left(\chi^{k}-\chi_{\infty}^{k}\right)\rangle}{k!}+f(p)\langle\frac{\rho^{n}}{n!},\frac{\chi^{n}}{n!}\rangle=0.

Hence

(6.57)

\displaystyle-f(p)\langle\frac{\rho^{n}}{n!},\frac{\chi^{n}}{n!}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\frac{\langle\Lambda^{[k]},\left(\chi^{k}-\chi_{\infty}^{k}\right)\rangle}{k!}.

We may write

(6.58)

A=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a&Q\\
Q^{\dagger}&(A|1)\end{array}\right),A^{-1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\hat{a}^{-1}&\hat{Q}\\
\hat{Q}^{\dagger}&(A|1)^{-1}+\hat{Q}^{\dagger}\hat{a}\hat{Q}\end{array}\right),

where
\hat{a}=a-Q(A|1)^{-1}Q^{\dagger}=1/\|dz^{1}\|_{\omega}^{2}

and
\hat{Q}=-\hat{a}^{-1}Q(A|1)^{-1}
. Let

(6.59)

\xi=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\hat{a}}}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}}+\sqrt{\hat{a}}\sum_{i=2}^{n}\hat{Q}_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{i}}.

Then

(6.60)

\displaystyle\chi

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{-1}\left(\xi\wedge\bar{\xi}+(A|1)^{\bar{j}i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}^{j}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{i}}\right),

Thus, we have

(6.61)

\exp\chi-\exp\chi_{\infty}=2\sqrt{-1}\xi\wedge\bar{\xi}\wedge\exp\chi_{\infty}.

By (
6.57
), (
6.61
), and Lemma
6.5
,
we have

(6.62)

\displaystyle-\frac{f(p)}{\det A}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\det A}\frac{|\langle\xi,dz^{1}\rangle|^{2}}{\|dz^{1}\|_{\omega}^{2}}m\gamma_{\min}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\det A}\cdot m\gamma_{\min}.

However, it is impossible as
|f(p)|<m\gamma_{\min}
. We have finished
the proof.

Finally, we show that
F
is strict convex in
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}^{\kappa}(p)
.
∎

Lemma 6.7
.

Notations as above. If
\mathring{\Lambda}

satisfies
\mathcal{O}
-UP then

(6.63)

\sum_{r,s,i,j}\left(F^{i\bar{j},r\bar{s}}(A)+F^{i\bar{s}}(A)A^{\bar{j}r}\right)B_{i\bar{j}}\overline{B_{s\bar{r}}}\geq\frac{f(p)}{\det A}A^{\bar{s}r}A^{\bar{j}i}B_{i\bar{j}}\overline{B_{s\bar{r}}}.

Suppose further that for all
p\in M
,
f(p)>-\frac{m}{2n+1}\gamma_{\min}(\frac{\kappa}{m},n_{p},\mathbf{d}_{p},\mathbf{k}_{p}).

Then,
F
is a strictly convex function in
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}^{\kappa}
.

Proof.

In a local normal coordinate of
\rho
at
p
, we may assume that

A=\text{diag}\{\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{n}\}
. By Proposition

4.3
,

(6.64)

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j,r,s}\left(F^{i\bar{j},r\bar{s}}+F^{i\bar{s}}A^{\bar{j}r}\right)B_{i\bar{j}}\overline{B_{s\bar{r}}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{f(p)}{\det A}\left(A^{\bar{s}r}A^{\bar{j}i}\right)B_{i\bar{j}}\overline{B_{s\bar{r}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{f(p)}{\det A}\left|\sum_{j}\frac{B_{j\bar{j}}}{\lambda_{j}}\right|^{2}.

By (
6.54
), we have

(6.65)

\displaystyle-\sum_{i,j,r,s}F^{i\bar{s}}(A)A^{\bar{j}r}B_{i\bar{j}}\overline{B_{s\bar{r}}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\det A}\left(\frac{m}{2}\gamma_{\min}(\frac{\kappa}{m},n_{p},\mathbf{d}_{p},\mathbf{k}_{p})+f(p)\right)\sum_{i,j}\frac{|B_{i\bar{j}}|^{2}}{\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}}.

Then by (
6.65
), Cauchy inequality, and the assumption
on
f
, we have

(6.66)

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j,r,s}\left(F^{i\bar{j},r\bar{s}}+\frac{1}{2}F^{i\bar{s}}A^{\bar{j}r}\right)B_{i\bar{j}}\overline{B_{s\bar{r}}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\det A}\sum_{j}\frac{|B_{j\bar{j}}|^{2}}{\lambda_{j}^{2}}\left(\frac{m}{2}\gamma_{\min}(\frac{\kappa}{m},n_{p},\mathbf{d}_{p},\mathbf{k}_{p})-(n+\frac{1}{2})|f(p)|\right)

\displaystyle\geq 0.

Since
F^{i\bar{j}}
is strictly monotone in
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}^{\kappa}

by Lemma
6.6
, by (
6.66
)

F^{i\bar{j},r\bar{s}}
is positive definite in
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}^{\kappa}
.
Thus,
F
is strictly convex in
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}^{\kappa}
.
∎

7.
Continuity method

In this section, we prove the existence of a solution to (
1.2
)
assuming the existence of a subsolution
\omega_{\text{sub}}\in[\omega_{0}].

The main result of this section is the following

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
M
be a connected compact Kähler
manifold. Suppose
\Lambda
satisfies
H2
. If there is a
Kähler metric
\omega_{\text{sub}}\in[\omega_{0}]
satisfies the
cone condition (
1.5
), then there exists a unique
smooth solution to equation (
1.2
).

Remark 7.2
.

Since a solution itself is a subsolution by Lemma
6.1
,
Theorem
1.7
and Theorem
2.9

are immediate corollaries of Theorem
7.1
.
The uniqueness will be addressed in Appendix
A
.

To prove Theorem
7.1
, we use the continuity
method. Consider the following continuity path depending on the parameter

t\in[0,1]
. Consider
\Omega_{t}=\exp\omega_{t}
which solves the
PDE

(7.1)

\kappa(\Omega_{t})^{[n]}=\left(t\mathring{\Lambda}\wedge\Omega_{t}\right)^{[n]}+(tf+\left(1-t\right)\kappa_{0})P^{[n]},

where the constant
\kappa_{0}
is chosen such that

\kappa[\omega_{0}]^{n}=\kappa_{0}[\rho]^{n}.

Denote
f_{t}(p):=tf(p)+(1-t)\kappa_{0}
, and
\Lambda_{t}=t\mathring{\Lambda}+f_{t}P^{[n]}
,
then we may rewrite (
7.1
) as

(7.2)

\kappa(\Omega_{t})^{[n]}=\left(\Lambda_{t}\wedge\Omega_{t}\right)^{[n]}.

Let

\mathbf{I}=\{t\in[0,1]:(\ref{eq:contin path})\ \text{has a smooth solution}\}.

Then, from Yau’s theorem
[
38
]
, there is a smooth
\rm{K\ddot{a}hler}

metric
\hat{\omega}_{0}
which solves (
7.2
)
at
t=0
. Without confusion, we replace
\omega_{0}
by
\hat{\omega}_{0}

and consider
\omega_{t}=\omega_{0}+i\partial\bar{\partial}\varphi_{t}
which solves
(
7.1
) for
t\in\mathbf{I}\subset[0,1]
. Notice
the linearization of (
7.1
) is

(7.3)

\left(\kappa\Omega_{t}-t\Lambda\wedge\Omega_{t}\right)^{[n-1]}\wedge i\partial\bar{\partial}u=(t\mathring{\Lambda}\wedge\Omega_{t})^{[n]}+\left(f(p)-\kappa_{0}\right)P^{[n]}.

By Lemma
6.1
,
\left(\kappa\Omega_{t}-t\Lambda\wedge\Omega_{t}\right)^{[n-1]}>0
;
Therefore, (
7.3
) is strictly elliptic, which implies the
openness of
\mathbf{I}
. If
\Lambda
satisfies
H2
, then
for
t>0
,
\Lambda_{t}
satisfies
H2
with respect to proper
positive constants. In fact,
f_{t}(p)\geq 0
if
t\leq\frac{1}{2}
,
and since
m_{t}\geq\frac{m}{2}
for
t\geq\frac{1}{2}
, we have

f_{t}(p)\geq-\min\left\{\frac{m_{t}}{2n+1}\gamma_{\min}(\frac{\kappa}{m_{t}},n_{p},\mathbf{d}_{p},\mathbf{k}_{p}),\frac{\kappa_{0}}{2}\right\}.

Thus, if
\Lambda
satisfies
H2,

\Lambda_{t}
satisfies
the following
H2’
condition.

Definition 7.3
.

We say
\Lambda
satisfies
H2’
if
\mathring{\Lambda}

satisfies
H2
with some uniform constant
m>0
, and
\Lambda^{[n]}

is almost positive with respect to
(\mathcal{O},2m,\rho)
, i.e.
for any
p\in M
,

\frac{\Lambda^{[n]}}{P^{[n]}}(p)\geq-\min\left\{\frac{m}{2n+1}\gamma_{\min}(\frac{\kappa}{m},n_{p},\mathbf{d}_{p},\mathbf{k}_{p}),\frac{\kappa_{0}}{2}\right\}.

Remark 7.4
.

Readers may check that all arguments in section
6

are valid if condition
H2’
is assumed.

In the following, by replacing
\Lambda
with
\Lambda_{t}
, we
will suppress the subscript
t
and derive a priori estimates for
equation (
1.2
) assuming
H2’
.

We proceed to prove a priori
C^{0}
-estimate of equation (
1.2
).
The idea of using Alexandroff-Bakelman-Pucci type estimates based
on
[
2
]
follows
[
35
]
.
Let

(7.4)

\omega=\omega_{0}+i\partial\bar{\partial}\varphi

be a solution to (
1.2
). Suppose that in the

\rm{K\ddot{a}hler}
class
[\omega_{0}]
there is a
\omega_{\text{sub}}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}^{\kappa}
.
We denote

(7.5)

\omega_{\text{sub}}=\omega_{0}+i\partial\bar{\partial}\varphi_{\text{sub}}

Let
u=\varphi-\varphi_{\text{sub}}
.

Proposition 7.5
.

Suppose that
\Lambda
satisfies
H2’
.
If
\omega=\omega_{\text{sub}}+i\partial\bar{\partial}u
is a solution to (
1.2
)
and
\sup u=0
, then there is a constant
C
depends on
n
,
M
,

\Lambda
,
\omega_{\text{sub}}
,
\rho
s.t.

\sup_{M}|u|<C.

Suppose that
\underline{A}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}^{\kappa}(p)
.
Since the
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}^{\kappa}(p)
is open,
\text{dist}(\underline{A},\partial\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}^{\kappa}(p))>0
.
Thus there is a
0<r<\text{dist}(\underline{A},\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}^{\kappa}(p))

s.t. the radius
r
ball
\mathcal{B}_{r}(\underline{A})
in Hermitian
matrix space is contained in
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}^{\kappa}(p)

. As a result, we have

Lemma 7.6
.

If
\underline{A}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}^{\kappa}(p)
, then there
is a constant
r=r(\underline{A},\Lambda)
s.t.
\underline{A}-r\text{Id}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}^{\kappa}(p)
.

Proof of Proposition
7.5
.

Since
\omega
is positive, we have

(7.6)

\Delta_{\rho}u>-\text{tr}_{\rho}\omega_{\text{sub}}>-C(\omega_{\text{sub}},\rho).

Thus, we can use Green function representation to obtain that
\|u\|_{L^{1}(\rho^{n})}<C(\omega_{\text{sub}},\rho)
.

Let
L=-\inf_{M}u
and assume that
L
is achieved at
x_{0}
.
Pick a normal coordinate of
\rho
at
x_{0}
. After a proper rescaling
of
\rho,
we may assume that the chosen coordinate exists in the
unit ball
B_{1}(0)\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
. For
x\in B_{1}(0)
, we pick
a uniform
r=r(\omega_{\text{sub}},\Lambda)
s.t.
\omega_{\text{sub}}-r\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}|x|^{2}

belongs to
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}^{\kappa}(x)
for all
x\in B_{1}(0)
.
Let
a>0
and
a<r/2
. Let
w=u+a|x|^{2}
. Note
w>-L+a
on
\partial B_{1}(0)
.
Define the following set:

W=\{x\in B_{1}(0):|Dw(x)|<a,\ w(y)\geq w(x)+Dw(x)\cdot(y-x)\}.

We use Alexandroff-Bakelman-Pucci maximum principle (Gilbarg-Trudinger
Lemma 9.2) to claim that

B_{a}(0)\subset Dw(W).

In
W
, we have
D^{2}w\geq 0
and hence

(7.7)

c(n)a^{2n}\leq\int_{W}\det(D^{2}w)\leq 2^{2n}\int_{W}\left(\det w_{i\bar{j}}\right)^{2}.

As
D^{2}w\geq 0
in
W
,
D^{2}u\geq-2a\text{Id}_{2n}
which implies
that
u_{i\bar{j}}+a\delta_{i\bar{j}}\geq 0
as a Hermitian matrix.
Since
a<r/2
,
\omega_{\text{sub}}-a\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}|x|^{2}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}^{\kappa}(x)
,
we apply Proposition
5.2
to
\omega_{\text{sub}}-a\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}|x|^{2}

to conclude that
|w_{i\bar{j}}|<R(\Lambda,\omega_{\text{sub}})
.
By (
7.7
), we have

(7.8)

c(n)a^{2n}\leq C(\Lambda,\omega_{\text{sub}},n)\text{vol}(W).

On the other hand, it is obvious that

(7.9)

\text{vol}(W)\leq\frac{\|w\|_{L^{1}}}{|L-a|}\leq\frac{C(\omega_{\text{sub}},\rho)}{|L-a|}.

Thus by (
7.8
) and (
7.9
),

L<C(\Lambda,\omega_{\text{sub}},\rho,n)\left(\frac{1}{a^{2n}}+1\right)<C(\Lambda,\omega_{\text{sub}},\rho,n).

∎

Next, we state a
C^{2}
-estimate for solutions to (
7.1
).

Proposition 7.7
.

Let
\omega_{\text{sub}}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}^{\kappa}
.
Let
u=\varphi-\varphi_{\text{sub}}
where
\varphi,\varphi_{\text{sub}}

are given in (
7.4
),(
7.5
) and
\omega=\omega_{\text{sub}}+\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}u

solves (
1.2
). If
\Lambda
satisfies
H2’
,
and
F^{i\bar{j},r\bar{s}},F^{i\bar{j}}
satisfies inequality (
6.63
)
then it holds that

|\partial\bar{\partial}u|_{\rho}<C.

where
C
depends on
\|u\|_{C^{0}},M,\Lambda,\omega_{\text{sub}}
,
n,m,\kappa,C_{H2}
,

\rho
.

We first prove some technical lemmas.

Lemma 7.8
.

Notations as above. We have

-F^{i\bar{j}}A_{i\bar{j}}\leq nF(A).

Proof.

Since each
F_{k}
is homogeneous of degree
-k
, we have

\displaystyle-\frac{\partial F(A)}{\partial A_{i\bar{j}}}A_{i\bar{j}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}kF_{k}(A)+\frac{nf}{\det A}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}nF_{k}(A)+\frac{nf}{\det A}

\displaystyle=nF(A).

∎

Let
\{z^{i}\}
be a normal coordinate at
p
. Pick the direction

\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}}
and denote
\Lambda_{,1},\Lambda_{,1\bar{1}}

to be the corresponding covariant derivatives of
\Lambda
with respect
to the Chern connection of
\rho
. We denote

(7.10)

\displaystyle F_{,1\bar{1}}

\displaystyle=F(A,\Lambda_{,1\bar{1}}),\ F_{,\bar{1}}^{i\bar{j}}=\frac{\partial F\left(A,\Lambda_{,\bar{1}}\right)}{\partial A_{i\bar{j}}},

(7.11)

\displaystyle F_{k,1\bar{1}}

\displaystyle=F_{k}(A,\Lambda_{,1\bar{1}}),\ F_{k,\bar{1}}^{i\bar{j}}=\frac{\partial F_{k}\left(A,\Lambda_{,\bar{1}}\right)}{\partial A_{i\bar{j}}}.

Lemma 7.9
.

Notations as above. If
\Lambda
satisfies
H2’
,
and
F(A)\geq\kappa
, we have

(7.12)

\left|F_{,1\bar{1}}\right|<C_{\ref{lem:tech2}}F(A),

(7.13)

\left|2\text{Re}\left(F_{k,\bar{1}}^{i\bar{j}}B_{i\bar{j}}\right)\right|\leq\frac{C_{\ref{lem:tech2}}}{\epsilon}F(A)+C_{\ref{lem:tech2}}\epsilon F^{i\bar{s}}A^{r\bar{j}}B_{i\bar{j}}\overline{B_{s\bar{r}}},

for some
C_{\ref{lem:tech2}}=C_{\ref{lem:tech2}}(\kappa,n,m,C_{H2})
.

Proof.

From the cone condition, Lemma
6.4
, and Newton-Maclaurin
inequality, we have

(7.14)

\langle\frac{\rho_{i}^{l_{i}}}{l_{i}!},\exp\chi\rangle\leq\binom{d_{i}}{l_{i}}\binom{d_{i}}{k_{i}}^{-\frac{l_{i}}{k_{i}}}(\frac{n\kappa}{m})^{\frac{l_{i}}{k_{i}}}.

By
H2’
, there is a constant
C_{H2}
s.t. for
k=1,\cdots,n-1
,
it holds

-C_{H2}\left(\Lambda^{[k]}+\sum_{l\in\boldsymbol{l}_{k}}\rho_{1}^{l_{1}}\cdots\rho_{n_{p}}^{l_{n_{p}}}\right)\leq\left(\Lambda^{[k]}\right)_{,1\bar{1}}\leq C_{H2}\left(\Lambda^{[k]}+\sum_{l\in\boldsymbol{l}_{k}}\rho_{1}^{l_{1}}\cdots\rho_{n_{p}}^{l_{n_{p}}}\right),

where
\boldsymbol{l}_{k}=\{(l_{1},\cdots,l_{n_{p}}):\sum_{i}l_{i}=k,\ l_{i}=0\ \text{or}\ l_{i}\geq k_{i}\}
.
Hence

(7.15)

\displaystyle|F_{k,1\bar{1}}|

\displaystyle\leq C_{H2}\left(F_{k}+\sum_{l\in\boldsymbol{l}_{k}}\prod_{i=1}^{n_{p}}\langle\frac{\rho_{i}^{l_{i}}}{l_{i}!},\exp\chi\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=C_{H2}\left(F_{k}+\sum_{l\in\boldsymbol{l}_{k}}C_{1}(\frac{\kappa}{m},n_{p},\mathbf{d}_{p},\mathbf{k}_{p})\right)

\displaystyle\leq C_{H2}\left(F_{k}+C_{2}(\frac{\kappa}{m},n_{p},\mathbf{d}_{p},\mathbf{k}_{p})\right)

By the cone condition and Lemma
6.4
,

(7.16)

\det A^{-1}\leq C_{3}(m,\kappa,n_{p},\mathbf{d}_{p},\mathbf{k}_{p}).

By (
7.15
) and (
7.16
), we have

(7.17)

\displaystyle\left|F_{,1\bar{1}}\right|

\displaystyle<\sum_{k}|F_{k,1\bar{1}}|+|f_{,1\bar{1}}|\frac{1}{\det A}

\displaystyle<C_{4}F(A),

for some
C_{4}=C_{4}(\kappa,m,n_{p},\mathbf{d}_{p},\mathbf{k}_{p},C_{H2})
.

We use
H2’
to deduce that

(7.18)

\left|\text{Re}\left(F_{k,\bar{1}}^{i\bar{j}}b_{i}\bar{b}_{j}\right)\right|\leq C_{H2}\left(-F_{k}^{i\bar{j}}b_{i}\bar{b}_{j}+\langle\sum_{l\in\boldsymbol{l}_{k}}\rho_{1}^{l_{1}}\cdots\rho_{n_{p}}^{l_{n_{p}}},\exp\chi\wedge 2\sqrt{-1}\overline{b^{\sharp}}\wedge b^{\sharp}\rangle\right).

From (
6.16
) in Lemma
6.3
, we have

(7.19)

\langle\sum_{l\in\boldsymbol{l}_{k}}\rho_{1}^{l_{1}}\cdots\rho_{n_{p}}^{l_{n_{p}}},\exp\chi\wedge 2\sqrt{-1}\overline{b^{\sharp}}\wedge b^{\sharp}\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{n_{p}}\sum_{l\in\boldsymbol{l}_{k,i}}\left(\prod_{j\not=i}\langle\frac{\rho_{j}^{l_{j}}}{l_{j}!},e^{\chi}\rangle\right)\langle\frac{\rho_{i}^{l_{i}}}{l_{i}!},e^{\chi}\wedge 2\sqrt{-1}\overline{b^{\sharp}}\wedge b^{\sharp}\rangle,

where
\boldsymbol{l}_{k,i}=\{(l_{1},\cdots,l_{n_{p}})\in\boldsymbol{l}_{k}:l_{i}\geq 1\}
.
By (
6.5
) in Lemma
6.2
and
Newton-Maclaurin inequality, for
l\geq k_{i}
and any positive
d_{i}\times d_{i}

Hermitian matrix
D
, we have

(7.20)

\displaystyle T_{l-1}(D)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sigma_{l-1}(D)}{\sigma_{k_{i}-1}(D)}T_{k_{i}-1}(D)

\displaystyle\leq C(d_{i},l,k_{i})\frac{\sigma_{k_{i}-1}(D)(\sigma_{k_{i}}(D))^{\frac{l-k_{i}}{k_{i}}}}{\sigma_{k_{i}-1}(D)}T_{k_{i}-1}(D)

\displaystyle=C(d_{i},l,k_{i})(\sigma_{k_{i}}(D))^{\frac{l-k_{i}}{k_{i}}}T_{k_{i}-1}(D).

We apply (
7.20
) to
A^{-1}|_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}

and use (
7.14
) to obtain

(7.21)

\langle\frac{\rho_{i}^{l-1}}{(l-1)!},\exp\chi\wedge 2\sqrt{-1}\overline{b^{\sharp}}\wedge b^{\sharp}\rangle\leq C_{5}(\frac{\kappa}{m},n_{p},\mathbf{d}_{p},\mathbf{k}_{p},l)\langle\frac{\rho_{i}^{k_{i}}}{k_{i}!},\exp\chi\wedge 2\sqrt{-1}\overline{b^{\sharp}}\wedge b^{\sharp}\rangle.

Thus, by (
7.18
), (
7.19
), and
(
7.21
), we have

(7.22)

\displaystyle\left|\text{Re}\left(F_{k,\bar{1}}^{i\bar{j}}b_{i}\bar{b}_{j}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq C_{H2}\left(-F_{k}^{i\bar{j}}b_{i}\bar{b}_{j}+C_{6}(\frac{\kappa}{m},n_{p},\mathbf{d}_{p},\mathbf{k}_{p})\sum_{i=1}^{n_{p}}\langle\frac{\rho_{i}^{k_{i}}}{k_{i}!},\exp\chi\wedge 2\sqrt{-1}\overline{b^{\sharp}}\wedge b^{\sharp}\rangle\right)

\displaystyle\leq-C_{7}F^{i\bar{j}}b_{i}\bar{b}_{j},

where
C_{7}=C_{7}(\kappa,m,n_{p},\mathbf{d}_{p},\mathbf{k}_{p},C_{H2})
.

Now for matrix
B
, by (
7.22
) and mean value
inequality,

(7.23)

\displaystyle\left|\text{Re}\left(F_{,1}^{i\bar{s}}A^{r\bar{j}}(A_{i\bar{j}}+\epsilon B_{i\bar{j}})\left(\overline{A_{s\bar{r}}+\epsilon B_{s\bar{r}}}\right)\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq-C_{9}F^{i\bar{s}}A^{r\bar{j}}(A_{i\bar{j}}+\epsilon B_{i\bar{j}})\left(\overline{A_{s\bar{r}}+\epsilon B_{s\bar{r}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{9}\left(F(A)-\epsilon^{2}F^{i\bar{s}}A^{r\bar{j}}B_{i\bar{j}}\overline{B_{s\bar{r}}}\right),

where
C_{9}=C_{9}(n,\kappa,m,n_{p},\mathbf{d}_{p},\mathbf{k}_{p},C_{H2})
.
As a result of (
7.23
), we have

\left|2\text{Re}\left(F_{,\bar{1}}^{i\bar{j}}B_{i\bar{j}}\right)\right|\leq\frac{C_{10}}{\epsilon}F(A)-C_{10}\epsilon F^{i\bar{s}}A^{r\bar{j}}B_{i\bar{j}}\overline{B_{s\bar{r}}},

where
C_{10}=C_{10}(n,\kappa,m,n_{p},\mathbf{d}_{p},\mathbf{k}_{p},C_{H2})
.

Finally, we choose

C_{\ref{lem:tech2}}=\max\{C_{2},C_{4},C_{10}:p\in M\}.

Notice that for a fixed labeled

orthogonal splitting
\mathcal{O}
,
the set
\{(n_{p},\mathbf{d}_{p},\mathbf{k}_{p}):p\in M\}
is finite.
Thus
C_{\ref{lem:tech2}}
has a uniform upper bound which only depends
on
\kappa,m,n
and
C_{H2}
.
∎

Lemma 7.10
.

Notations as above. If
\Lambda
satisfies
H2’
,
and
F(A)\geq\kappa
, there is a constant
C_{\ref{lem:ddbar F}}

depends on
C_{\ref{lem:tech2}}
, and the bisectional curvature
\text{Rm}_{\rho}

of
\rho
s.t. for any
\epsilon>0

\displaystyle\partial_{1\bar{1}}F(A)

\displaystyle\geq A_{1\bar{1}}F^{i\bar{j}}\left(\log A_{1\bar{1}}\right)_{,i\bar{j}}+A^{\bar{1}1}F^{i\bar{j}}A_{i\bar{1},1}\overline{A_{j\bar{1},1}}+F^{i\bar{j},r\bar{s}}A_{i\bar{j},1}A_{r\bar{s},\bar{1}}

\displaystyle+\epsilon F^{i\bar{s}}A^{\bar{j}r}A_{i\bar{j},1}\overline{A_{s\bar{r},1}}-\frac{C_{\ref{lem:ddbar F}}}{\epsilon}F(A)+C_{\ref{lem:ddbar F}}A_{1\bar{1}}\sum_{i}F^{i\bar{i}}.

Proof.

We have

(7.24)

\partial_{1\bar{1}}F(A)=F^{i\bar{j}}A_{i\bar{j},1\bar{1}}+F^{i\bar{j},r\bar{s}}A_{i\bar{j},1}A_{r\bar{s},\bar{1}}+2\text{Re}\left(F_{,\bar{1}}^{i\bar{j}}A_{i\bar{j},1}\right)+F_{1\bar{1}}.

Note

(7.25)

F^{i\bar{j}}A_{i\bar{j},1\bar{1}}=F^{i\bar{j}}A_{1\bar{1},i\bar{j}}+F^{i\bar{j}}(\rho^{a\bar{b}}A_{a\bar{j}}R_{1\bar{1},i\bar{b}}-\rho^{a\bar{b}}A_{a\bar{1}}R_{i\bar{j},1\bar{b}}),

where
R_{i\bar{j},k\bar{l}}
is the bisectional curvature of
\rho
.
By (
7.24
) and (
7.25
),

(7.26)

\displaystyle\partial_{1\bar{1}}F(A)

\displaystyle=F^{i\bar{j}}A_{1\bar{1},i\bar{j}}+F^{i\bar{j}}\left(\rho^{a\bar{b}}A_{a\bar{j}}R_{1\bar{1},i\bar{b}}-\rho^{a\bar{b}}A_{a\bar{1}}R_{i\bar{j},1\bar{b}}\right)

\displaystyle+F^{i\bar{j},r\bar{s}}A_{i\bar{j},1}A_{r\bar{s},\bar{1}}+2\text{Re}\left(F_{,\bar{1}}^{i\bar{j}}A_{i\bar{j},1}\right)+F_{1\bar{1}}.

The first term in (
7.26
) is

(7.27)

F^{i\bar{j}}A_{1\bar{1},i\bar{j}}=A_{1\bar{1}}F^{i\bar{j}}\left(\log A_{1\bar{1}}\right)_{,i\bar{j}}+A^{\bar{1}1}F^{i\bar{j}}A_{i\bar{1},1}\overline{A_{j\bar{1},1}}.

The second term in (
7.26
) is controlled by

(7.28)

\displaystyle F^{i\bar{j}}\left(\rho^{a\bar{b}}A_{a\bar{j}}R_{1\bar{1},i\bar{b}}-\rho^{a\bar{b}}A_{a\bar{1}}R_{i\bar{j},1\bar{b}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq C_{1}F^{i\bar{j}}A_{i\bar{j}}+C_{1}A_{1\bar{1}}\sum_{i}F^{i\bar{i}}

\displaystyle\geq-C_{2}F(A)+C_{2}A_{1\bar{1}}\sum_{i}F^{i\bar{i}},

where the constants
C_{1},C_{2}
depend a bound of the bisectional
curvature
|\text{Rm}_{\rho}|
. Apply Lemma
7.9
to (
7.26
)
to obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{1\bar{1}}F(A)

\displaystyle\geq A_{1\bar{1}}F^{i\bar{j}}\left(\log A_{1\bar{1}}\right)_{,i\bar{j}}+A^{\bar{1}1}F^{i\bar{j}}A_{i\bar{1},1}\overline{A_{j\bar{1},1}}+F^{i\bar{j},r\bar{s}}A_{i\bar{j},1}A_{r\bar{s},\bar{1}}

\displaystyle+\epsilon F^{i\bar{s}}A^{\bar{j}r}A_{i\bar{j},1}\overline{A_{s\bar{r},1}}-C_{3}\left(1+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right)F(A)+C_{3}A_{1\bar{1}}\sum_{i}F^{i\bar{i}}.

We have proved the claim.
∎

The following proposition is a key ingredient in the proof of
C^{2}

estimate. It has been proved in several context. See Song-Weinkove

[
33
]
, Fang-Lai-Ma
[
19
]
, Guan

[
17
]
, Guan-Sun
[
18
]
, Collins-Szèkelyhidi

[
10
]
, Szèkelyhidi
[
35
]
,
Datar-Pingali
[
12
]
. The current form of Proposition

7.11
is adapted from

Fang-Lai-Ma
[
19
]

and Datar-Pingali
[
12
]
. We will put the proof
in the appendix.

Proposition 7.11
.

Suppose that
\Lambda
satisfies
H2’
.
Let
\omega_{\text{sub}}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}^{\kappa}
and
\omega=\omega_{\text{sub}}+i\partial\bar{\partial}u

be a solution to (
1.1
). Then there is
a
N=N(\omega_{\text{sub}},\Lambda,M)
and
\mu=\mu(\omega_{\text{sub}},\Lambda,M)>0

s.t. if
|\partial\bar{\partial}u|_{\rho}>N
,

F^{i\bar{j}}(A)\left(u_{i\bar{j}}\right)\geq\mu\left(1-\sum_{i}F^{i\bar{i}}(A)\right).

Now with all the preparations, we prove the
C^{2}
-estimate.

Proof of Proposition
7.7
.

We use maximum principle to deduce Proposition
7.7
.
Let
u=\varphi-\varphi_{\text{sub}}
and
g
be the metric tensor
of
\omega
. We may assume that
\inf_{M}u=0
,
|\partial\bar{\partial}u|>N
so
that we can apply Proposition
7.11
. Pick the
test function

(7.29)

G(x,\xi)=\log(g_{i\bar{j}}\xi^{i}\xi^{\bar{j}})-\phi(u),

where
\xi\in T_{x}^{1,0}M,\rho_{i\bar{j}}\xi^{i}\xi^{\bar{j}}=1
.

\phi:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}
is a smooth function:

(7.30)

\phi(x)=2Lx-\frac{L\tau}{2}x^{2}.

The choice of
\tau
relies on
\sup u
so that

(7.31)

L\leq\phi^{\prime}\leq 2L,\ \phi^{\prime\prime}=-L\tau.

For instance, we may start by assuming that
\tau=\frac{1}{\sup u+1}
.
Suppose that
G(x,\xi)
achieves maximum at
(p,\xi_{0})
. We choose
a normal coordinate
\{z^{i}\}
of
\rho
at
p
so that
\xi_{0}

is along the direction of
\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}}
and
\omega

is diagonal at
p
. Locally

(7.32)

H=\log g_{1\bar{1}}-\phi(u)

also achieves maximum at
p
. At
p
, we have

(7.33)

0=H_{,i}=g_{1\bar{1}}^{-1}g_{1\bar{1},i}-\phi^{\prime}u_{,i},

(7.34)

0\leq F^{i\bar{j}}H_{,i\bar{j}}.

At
p
, we have

(7.35)

0=H_{,i}=g_{1\bar{1}}^{-1}g_{1\bar{1},i}-\phi^{\prime}u_{,i}.

We have
g_{1\bar{1},i}=g_{i\bar{1},1}
and (
7.35
) implies
that

(7.36)

u_{,i}=\frac{g^{\bar{1}1}}{\phi^{\prime}}g_{i\bar{1},1}.

Now

(7.37)

H_{,i\bar{j}}=\left(\log g_{1\bar{1}}\right)_{,i\bar{j}}-\phi^{\prime\prime}u_{,i}u_{,\bar{j}}-\phi^{\prime}u_{,i\bar{j}}.

We have
g_{1\bar{1},i}=g_{i\bar{1},1}
. Thus, by Lemma
7.10
,

(7.38)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq F^{i\bar{j}}H_{,i\bar{j}}=F^{i\bar{j}}\left(\left(\log g_{1\bar{1}}\right)_{,i\bar{j}}-\phi^{\prime\prime}u_{,i}u_{,\bar{j}}-\phi^{\prime}u_{,i\bar{j}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq-g^{\bar{1}1}\left(F^{i\bar{j},r\bar{s}}g_{i\bar{j},1}g_{r\bar{s},\bar{1}}+F^{i\bar{j}}g^{\bar{1}1}g_{i\bar{1},1}\overline{g_{j\bar{1},1}}+\epsilon F^{i\bar{j}}g^{\bar{l}l}g_{i\bar{l},1}\overline{g_{j\bar{l},1}}\right)-\frac{C_{\ref{lem:ddbar F}}}{\epsilon}F(A)

\displaystyle\ -C_{\ref{lem:ddbar F}}\sum_{i}F^{i\bar{i}}-F^{i\bar{j}}\left(\phi^{\prime\prime}u_{,i}u_{,\bar{j}}+\phi^{\prime}u_{,i\bar{j}}\right).

We apply (
6.63
) to (
7.38
)
to get

(7.39)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq g^{\bar{1}1}\left((1-\epsilon)F^{i\bar{j}}g^{\bar{l}l}g_{i\bar{l},1}\overline{g_{j\bar{l},1}}-\frac{f(p)}{\det g}\left|\sum_{j}g^{\bar{j}j}g_{j\bar{j},1}\right|^{2}-F^{i\bar{j}}g^{\bar{1}1}g_{i\bar{1},1}\overline{g_{j\bar{1},1}}\right)

\displaystyle+g^{\bar{1}1}\frac{C_{\ref{lem:ddbar F}}}{\epsilon}F-C_{\ref{lem:ddbar F}}\sum_{i}F^{i\bar{i}}-F^{i\bar{j}}\left(\phi^{\prime\prime}u_{,i}u_{,\bar{j}}+\phi^{\prime}u_{,i\bar{j}}\right).

Substitute
u_{,i}=\frac{g^{\bar{1}1}}{\phi^{\prime}}g_{i\bar{1},1}
into
(
7.39
) to get

(7.40)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq(1-\epsilon)g^{\bar{1}1}F^{i\bar{j}}g^{\bar{l}l}g_{i\bar{l},1}\overline{g_{j\bar{l},1}}-\frac{f(p)}{\det g}\left|\sum_{j}g^{\bar{j}j}g_{j\bar{j},1}\right|^{2}-F^{i\bar{j}}(g^{\bar{1}1})^{2}g_{i\bar{1},1}\overline{g_{j\bar{1},1}}

\displaystyle+g^{\bar{1}1}\frac{C_{\ref{lem:ddbar F}}}{\epsilon}F(A)-\frac{\phi^{\prime\prime}}{(\phi^{\prime})^{2}}F^{i\bar{j}}\left(g^{\bar{1}1}\right)^{2}g_{i\bar{1},1}\overline{g_{j\bar{1},1}}-C_{\ref{lem:ddbar F}}\sum_{i}F^{i\bar{i}}-\phi^{\prime}F^{i\bar{j}}u_{,i\bar{j}}

\displaystyle=g^{\bar{1}1}\left[(1-\epsilon)F^{i\bar{j}}g^{\bar{l}l}g_{i\bar{l},1}\overline{g_{j\bar{l},1}}-\frac{f(p)}{\det g}\left|\sum_{j}g^{\bar{j}j}g_{j\bar{j},1}\right|^{2}-\left(1+\frac{\phi^{\prime\prime}}{(\phi^{\prime})^{2}}\right)F^{i\bar{j}}g^{\bar{1}1}g_{i\bar{1},1}\overline{g_{j\bar{1},1}}\right]

\displaystyle+g^{\bar{1}1}\frac{C_{\ref{lem:ddbar F}}}{\epsilon}F-C_{\ref{lem:ddbar F}}\sum_{i}F^{i\bar{i}}-\phi^{\prime}F^{i\bar{j}}u_{,i\bar{j}}.

Take
\epsilon\leq\min\{-\frac{\phi^{\prime\prime}}{(\phi^{\prime})^{2}},\frac{1}{2}\}
,
then we have

(7.41)

\displaystyle(1-\epsilon)F^{i\bar{j}}g^{\bar{l}l}g_{i\bar{l},1}\overline{g_{j\bar{l},1}}-\left(1+\frac{\phi^{\prime\prime}}{(\phi^{\prime})^{2}}\right)F^{i\bar{j}}g^{\bar{1}1}g_{i\bar{1},1}\overline{g_{j\bar{1},1}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{l\geq 2}F^{i\bar{j}}g^{\bar{l}l}g_{i\bar{l},1}\overline{g_{j\bar{l},1}}.

If
f(p)\geq 0
, then by (
7.41
), (
7.41
), and the fact
that
F^{i\bar{j}}\leq 0
in Lemma
6.6
,

(7.42)

0\leq g^{\bar{1}1}\frac{C_{\ref{lem:ddbar F}}}{\epsilon}F-C_{\ref{lem:ddbar F}}\sum_{i}F^{i\bar{i}}-\phi^{\prime}F^{i\bar{j}}u_{,i\bar{j}}.

If
f(p)<0
, then by (
6.54
), we have

(7.43)

-\sum_{l\geq 2}F^{i\bar{j}}g^{\bar{l}l}g_{i\bar{l},1}\overline{g_{j\bar{l},1}}\geq\left(\frac{m}{2}\gamma_{\min}(\frac{\kappa}{m},n_{p},\mathbf{d}_{p},\mathbf{k}_{p})-|f(p)|\right)\frac{1}{\det g}\sum_{l\geq 2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{|g_{i\bar{l},1}|^{2}}{g_{l\bar{l}}g_{i\bar{i}}}.

We argue similarly as in Lemma
6.7
:

(7.44)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{l\geq 2}F^{i\bar{j}}g^{\bar{l}l}g_{i\bar{l},1}\overline{g_{j\bar{l},1}}-\frac{f(p)}{\det g}\left|\sum_{j}g^{\bar{j}j}g_{j\bar{j},1}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{1}{\det g}\left(\left(\frac{m}{4}\gamma_{\min}(\frac{\kappa}{m},n_{p},\mathbf{d}_{p},\mathbf{k}_{p})-\frac{1}{2}|f(p)|\right)\sum_{l\geq 2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{|g_{i\bar{l},1}|^{2}}{g_{l\bar{l}}g_{i\bar{i}}}-|f(p)|n\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{|g_{j\bar{j},1}|^{2}}{g_{j\bar{j}}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{n|f(p)|}{\det g}\frac{|g_{1\bar{1},1}|^{2}}{g_{1\bar{1}}^{2}}.

The last line is due to the almost positive volume condition.

By (
7.40
), (
7.41
), (
7.44
), and (
7.42
),
we have

(7.45)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{\min\{0,f(p)\}n(g^{\bar{1}1})^{3}}{\det g}|g_{1\bar{1},1}|^{2}+g^{\bar{1}1}\frac{C_{\ref{lem:ddbar F}}}{\epsilon}F-C_{\ref{lem:ddbar F}}\sum_{i}F^{i\bar{i}}-\phi^{\prime}F^{i\bar{j}}u_{,i\bar{j}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}(m,\kappa,n)(g^{\bar{1}1})^{3}|g_{1\bar{1},1}|^{2}+g^{\bar{1}1}\frac{C_{\ref{lem:ddbar F}}}{\epsilon}F-C_{\ref{lem:ddbar F}}\sum_{i}F^{i\bar{i}}-\phi^{\prime}F^{i\bar{j}}u_{,i\bar{j}},

where the last line is due to
\det g^{-1}\leq C_{1}(m,\kappa,n_{p},\mathbf{d}_{p},\mathbf{k}_{p})

by Lemma
6.4
. Since
u_{,i\bar{j}}=g_{i\bar{j}}-g_{i\bar{j}}^{\text{sub}},

if
|\partial\bar{\partial}u|>N
, from Proposition
7.11
, we
have

(7.46)

\displaystyle F^{i\bar{j}}\left(u_{,i\bar{j}}\right)

\displaystyle=F^{i\bar{j}}\left(g_{i\bar{j}}-g_{i\bar{j}}^{\text{sub}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\mu(1-\sum_{i}F^{i\bar{i}}).

Thus, use
7.45
) and
g_{1\bar{1},1}=\phi^{\prime}u_{,1}g_{1\bar{1}}

to get

(7.47)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}g^{\bar{1}1}(\phi^{\prime})^{2}\left(1+|\nabla u|^{2}\right)+g^{\bar{1}1}\frac{C_{\ref{lem:ddbar F}}}{\epsilon}F(A)-C_{\ref{lem:ddbar F}}\sum_{i}F^{i\bar{i}}-\phi^{\prime}\mu(1-\sum_{i}F^{i\bar{i}})

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}g^{\bar{1}1}(\phi^{\prime})^{2}\left(1+|\nabla u|^{2}\right)+g^{\bar{1}1}\frac{C_{\ref{lem:ddbar F}}}{\epsilon}F(A)-\phi^{\prime}\mu+\left(\phi^{\prime}\mu-C_{\ref{lem:ddbar F}}\right)\sum_{i}F^{i\bar{i}}.

We choose
\phi^{\prime}\mu>C_{\ref{lem:ddbar F}}
and then by (
7.47
)
,

(7.48)

0\leq C_{1}g^{\bar{1}1}(\phi^{\prime})^{2}\left(1+|\nabla u|^{2}\right)+g^{\bar{1}1}\frac{C_{\ref{lem:ddbar F}}}{\epsilon}F(A)-\phi^{\prime}\mu.

Therefore, by (
7.48
), we have
g_{1\bar{1}}\leq C_{2}(1+|\nabla u|^{2}),

and hence at
p

(7.49)

|\partial\bar{\partial}u|<C_{3}(1+|\nabla u|^{2}),

for some
C_{3}=C_{3}(C_{\ref{lem:ddbar F}},L,\epsilon,\mu,m,\kappa,n)
.

Now, we fix choices of
L,\tau,\epsilon
. First, we need
\phi^{\prime}\mu>C_{\ref{lem:ddbar F}}

which requires that
L>\frac{C_{\ref{lem:ddbar F}}}{\mu}+1
. Next,
we need
\epsilon\leq-\frac{\phi^{\prime\prime}}{(\phi^{\prime})^{2}}.
Note

(7.50)

-\frac{\phi^{\prime\prime}}{(\phi^{\prime})^{2}}\geq\frac{L\tau}{4L^{2}}=\frac{\tau}{4L}.

Therefore, we may pick
\epsilon=\min\{\frac{\tau}{4L},\frac{1}{2}\}=\frac{1}{4L(\sup u+1)}
.
Note
L,\epsilon,\tau
only depend on
M,\Lambda,\omega_{\text{sub}},n,m,\kappa,
C_{H2},\sup|u|,|\text{Rm}_{\rho}|
.

Once we have arranged
L,\epsilon,\tau
properly, from (
7.49
),

(7.51)

|\partial\bar{\partial}u|<C_{4}\sup(|\nabla u|^{2}+1),

for some
C_{4}=C_{4}(M,\Lambda,\omega_{\text{sub}},n,m,\kappa,
C_{H2},\sup|u|,|\text{Rm}_{\rho}|)
.
Finally, we apply the blow-up technique in
[
8
]
(Proposition
5.1) to (
7.51
) to deduce that
|\partial\bar{\partial}u|\leq C_{5}(M,\Lambda,\omega_{\text{sub}},n,
m,\kappa,C_{H2},\sup|u|,|\text{Rm}_{\rho}|)

as desired.
∎

We now give the proof of Theorem
7.1
.

Proof of Theorem
7.1
.

Suppose that
\Lambda
satisfies
H2
with uniform constant

m>0
. From discussions above, we only need to show that
\mathbf{I}

is closed. Since
\mathbf{I}
is open
(0,t_{0})\subset\mathbf{I}

for some
t_{0}>0
. Suppose that
t_{1}=\sup\{t:[0,t)\subset\mathbf{I}\}>t_{0}>0
.
Let
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
, and denote

\Lambda_{t}=t\mathring{\Lambda}+f_{t}P^{[n]}.

Then as explained before,
\Lambda_{t}
satisfies
H2’
. It
is straightforward to see that if
\omega_{\text{sub}}
is a subsolution
for (
1.2
), then it is a subsolution for (
7.1
)
at all
t\in[0,1]
. So we can apply the a priori
C^{0}
,
C^{2}

estimates derived in Proposition
7.5
and
7.7
.

C^{1}
estimate is obtained by the blow-up technique as in
[
8
]

(Proposition 5.1). Together with the complex version of Evans-Krylov
theory we obtain
C^{2,\alpha}
bounds for solutions of (
7.1
)
for all
t\in\mathbf{I}
. The standard Schauder theory implies
C^{k,\alpha}

bounds for every
k
. The closedness of
\mathbf{I}
can then be
obtained from Arzela-Ascoli theorem. Thus,
\mathbf{I}
is closed
which implies the existence of smooth solution of (
1.2
).
∎

Part II
Numerical Criterion

The second part of this paper aims to give a proof of Theorem
1.10
.
The main approach to prove Theorem
1.10

is an induction argument on dimension introduced first by G. Chen

[
4
]
. A key component is the so called mass concentration
technique, which was originally due to Demailly-Păun
[
14
]

and has been successfully employed by Chen
[
4
]
to the

J
-equation and the supercritical dHYM equation. Our work is also
based on Song
[
32
]
, in which Song improved
Chen’s argument to treat singular sub-varieties.

The rest of this part is organized as follows. In Section
9
,
we set up the above-mentioned induction process and establish the
base case. In Section
10
, we prove a mass
concentration result, Theorem
10.1
. In Section
11
,
we complete the induction argument, and prove Theorem
1.10
.
In Section
12
, we apply Theorem

1.10
to study special cases of dHYM equations.

8.
Notations and technical preparation

In this section, we set up some further notations and definitions.
Assume that
M
,
[\omega_{0}]
,
\Lambda
,
\kappa
are given
as before.

We first define regularized maximum functions. Readers may consult

[
13
]
I.5.E. for general discussion.

Definition 8.1
.

(Regularized Maximum). For any
\eta\in(0,\infty)^{l}
, the regularized
maximum function is defined as

\widetilde{\max}_{\eta}(t_{1},\cdots,t_{l}):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{l}}\max(t_{1}+h_{1},\cdots,t_{l}+h_{l})\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}\theta\left(\frac{h_{j}}{\eta_{j}}\right)\frac{dh_{1}\cdots dh_{l}}{\eta_{1}\cdots\eta_{l}},

for
\eta=(\eta_{1},\cdots,\eta_{l})\in\left(0,\infty\right)^{l}
.
Here
\theta
is a smooth non-negative function supported on
(-1,1)

s.t.
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\theta(t)dt=1
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}}t\theta(t)dt=0
.

We will use the regularized maximum function and Richberg’s technique

[
30
]
to glue local PSH functions. Some related
known facts are collected in the following lemma. See
[
13
]

I.5.18 for proofs.

Lemma 8.2
.

For any
\eta\in(0,\infty)^{l}
,
\widetilde{\max}_{\eta}

possesses the following properties:

(1)

\widetilde{\max}_{\eta}(t_{1},\cdots,t_{l})

is non-decreasing in all variables, smooth and convex on
\mathbb{R}^{l}
;

(2)

\max\{t_{1},\cdots,t_{l}\}\leq\widetilde{\max}_{\eta}(t_{1},\cdots,t_{l})\leq\max\{t_{1}+\eta_{1},\cdots,t_{l}+\eta_{l}\}
;

(3)

if
\ t_{j}+\eta_{j}\leq\max_{k\not=j}\{t_{k}-\eta_{k}\}
,
then

\widetilde{\max}_{\eta}(t_{1},\cdots,t_{l})=\widetilde{\max}_{(\eta_{1},\cdots,\hat{\eta}_{j},\cdots,t_{l})}(t_{1},\cdots,\hat{t}_{j},\cdots,t_{l});

(4)

\widetilde{\max}_{\eta}(t_{1}+a,\cdots,t_{l}+a)=\widetilde{\max}_{\eta}(t_{1},\cdots,t_{l})+a
.

We prove the following technical lemma.

Lemma 8.3
.

If
g=g(A_{1},\cdots,A_{l})

is a convex function on the spaces of Hermitian matrices and is monotone
decreasing in each
A_{i}
, then

(8.1)

g(\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}\widetilde{\max}_{\eta}(u_{1},\cdots,u_{l}))\leq\sum_{i}\frac{\partial\widetilde{\max}_{\eta}}{\partial u_{i}}g(\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}u_{i}).

In particular, if
u_{1},\cdots,u_{l}
are in
\text{PSH}(M,\omega_{0})

and satisfies
\mathcal{P}_{\Lambda}(\omega_{0}+\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}u_{i})<\kappa,

then

(8.2)

\mathcal{P}_{\Lambda}(\omega_{0}+\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}\widetilde{\max}_{\eta}(u_{1},\cdots,u_{l}))\leq\mathcal{P}_{\Lambda}(\omega_{0}+\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}u_{i})<\kappa.

Proof.

Suppose that
u_{1},\cdots,u_{l}
are
C^{2}
functions on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
Then

(8.3)

\displaystyle\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}\widetilde{\max}_{\eta}(u_{1},\cdots,u_{l})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\frac{\partial\widetilde{\max}_{\eta}}{\partial u_{i}}\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}u_{i}+\sum_{i,j}\frac{\partial^{2}\widetilde{\max}_{\eta}}{\partial u_{i}\partial u_{j}}\sqrt{-1}\partial u_{j}\wedge\bar{\partial}u_{i}.

From Lemma
8.2
(
4
), we see
that
\sum_{i}\frac{\partial\widetilde{\max}_{\eta}}{\partial u_{i}}=1
and
each
\frac{\partial\widetilde{\max}}{\partial u_{i}}
is non-negative. Thus,
from (
8.3
),

\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}\widetilde{\max}_{\eta}(u_{1},\cdots,u_{l})\geq\sum_{i}\frac{\partial\widetilde{\max}_{\eta}}{\partial u_{i}}\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}u_{i}.

The right hand side is a convex combination of
\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}u_{i}
.
Thus, (
8.1
) holds by the monotonicity and the
convexity of
g
. (
8.2
) holds by the corresponding
properties of
\mathcal{P}_{\Lambda}
in Lemma
5.10
.
∎

We use Richberg technique (
[
13
]
, I. Corollary
5.19) to patch up PSH functions on manifolds.

Corollary 8.4
.

Let
u_{\alpha}\in C^{\infty}(\overline{U}_{\alpha})\cap PSH(U_{\alpha},\omega)

where
U_{\alpha}\subset\subset M
is a finite open covering of
M
.
Assume that
u_{\beta}<\max\{u_{\alpha}(z)\}
at every point
z\in\partial U_{\beta}

when
\alpha
runs over the indices s.t.
z\in U_{\alpha}
. Choose
a family
\{\eta_{\alpha}\}
of positive numbers so small that
u_{\beta}(z)+\eta_{\beta}\leq\max_{U_{\alpha}\ni z}\{u_{\alpha}-\eta_{\alpha}\}

for all
\beta
s.t.
z\in\partial U_{\beta}
. Then the function

\tilde{u}(z)=\widetilde{\max}_{(\eta_{\alpha})}(u_{\alpha}(z))

is in
C^{\infty}(M)\cap\text{PSH}(M,\omega)
.

Next, we recall some well known definitions in complex geometry.

Definition 8.5
.

Let
T
be a closed positive
(1,1)
-current on
M
. We call
T

a
\rm{K\ddot{a}hler}
current if

T-\epsilon\gamma\geq 0,

for some
\epsilon>0
and
\gamma
is a Hermitian metric on
M
.

We recall the definition of Lelong number:

Definition 8.6
.

For
p\in B_{R}\subset V\subset\mathbb{C}^{d}
, and
\varphi\in\text{PSH}(V)
,
define

\nu_{\varphi}(p,r)=\frac{\bar{\varphi}(p,R)-\bar{\varphi}(p,r)}{\log R-\log r}

where
0<r<R
, and
\bar{\varphi}(p,r)=\sup_{B_{r}(p)}\varphi(z)
.
The Lelong number of
\varphi
at
p
is given by

\nu_{\varphi}(p)=\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\nu_{\varphi}(p,r).

The Lelong number of a closed positive
(1,1)
-current
T
, denoted
as
\nu_{T}(p)
, is defined to be the Lelong number of the local
potential function.

The classic result of Y.-T. Siu shows that the Lelong number is upper
semi-continuous with respect to analytic Zariski topology.

Theorem 8.7

(Siu’s semi-continuity theorem
[
31
]
)
.

If
T
is a closed positive
(1,1)
-current on
M
, then the upper
level sets

E_{c}(T)=\{p:\nu_{T}(p)\geq c\}

are analytic subsets of
M
of dimension
\leq n-1
.

Finally we define the local regularization of a current.

Definition 8.8
.

Let
T
be a closed
positive
(1,1)
-current on a smooth variety
Y
of dimension
d

and
R>0
. We call
T^{(r)}=\{T_{j}^{(r)}\}_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
a
local regularization of
T
with respect to a finite open covering

\mathscr{P}=\{B_{j,3R}\}_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
of
Y
if the following
conditions (1) and (2) are satisfied.

(1)

Each
B_{j,3R}
is biholomorphic to a Euclidean ball
B_{3R}(0)

in
\mathbb{C}^{d}
equipped with standard Euclidean metric
g_{j}
. Furthermore,

B_{j,R}\simeq B_{R}(0)\subset\mathbb{C}^{d}
is also a covering of
Y
;

(2)

T_{j}^{(r)}(z)
is the standard smoothing of
T
in
B_{j,2R}

defined by

T_{j}^{(r)}(z)=\int_{B_{r}(0)}r^{-2m}\vartheta\left(\frac{z^{\prime}}{|r|}\right)T(z-z^{\prime})dV_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}(z^{\prime})

for
r\in(0,R)
.
\vartheta(t)
is a smooth non-negative function
with support in
[0,1]
satisfying
\vartheta\equiv\text{const}

in
[0,1/2]
and a normalization condition:

\int_{B_{1}(0)}\vartheta(|z^{\prime}|)dV_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}(z^{\prime})=1.

If in each
B_{3R}
,
T=\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}\varphi_{i}
for some local
PSH function
\varphi_{i}
. Then it is easy to see that for
r\in(0,R)
,

T_{j}^{(r)}=\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}\varphi_{i}^{(r)},

where

\varphi_{i}^{(r)}(z)=\int_{B_{r}(0)}r^{-2m}\vartheta\left(\frac{z^{\prime}}{|r|}\right)\varphi_{i}(z-z^{\prime})dV_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}(z^{\prime}).

9.
Initiation of induction argument

In this section, we state the main technical theorem of this part
and initiate an induction proof. Several technical issues including
the resolution of singularities are also discussed.

The following theorem is the main goal of the rest of this part:

Theorem 9.1
.

Let
M
be a
n
-dimensional
connected compact Kähler manifold. Let
\kappa>0
be a constant.
Let
\Lambda
be a close real form satisfies
H1
. If
[\omega_{0}]

is
([\Lambda],\kappa)
-positive, then for any analytic subvariety

Y
with dimension
d\leq n
, there is a neighborhood
U_{Y}
of

Y
in
M
and a Kähler form
\omega_{U_{Y}}\in[\omega_{0}]|_{U_{Y}}

satisfies the cone condition (
1.5
) on
U_{Y}
.

Without loss of generality, by a rescaling of
\Lambda
, we may assume
that
\kappa=1
in the rest of this part. Then the corresponding
cone condition is

(9.1)

((1-\Lambda)\wedge\exp\omega)^{[n-1]}>0\quad\text{or}\quad\mathcal{P}_{\Lambda}(\omega)<1.

Note Theorem
1.10
, presented in our introduction,
follows immediately from Theorem
9.1

and Theorem
7.1
.

Remark 9.2
.

If
Y
is a smooth subvariety of
M
, Theorem
9.1

implies that
\omega_{Y}=\omega_{U}|_{Y}
satisfies the cone condition
on
Y
:

(9.2)

(\exp\omega_{Y})^{[\dim Y-1]}>(\Lambda|_{Y}\wedge\exp\omega_{Y})^{[\dim Y-1]}\quad\text{or}\quad\mathcal{P}_{\Lambda|_{Y}}(\omega_{Y})<1.

On the other hand, (
9.2
) also implies the existence
of a subsolution in a neighborhood
U
of a smooth subvariety
Y
.
This is stated in the following lemma.

Lemma 9.3
.

Notations as above. Suppose
that
Y
is a smooth subvariety of
M
and
\omega_{Y}\in[\omega_{0}|_{Y}]

satisfies the cone condition (
9.2
) on
Y
. Then
there exists a neighborhood
U
of
Y
in
M
and a Kähler form

\omega_{U}\in[\omega_{0}|_{U}]
such that
\omega_{U}|_{Y}=\omega_{Y}

and
\omega_{U}
satisfies the cone condition (
9.1
)
in
U
.

Proof.

Let
U_{1}
be a tubular neighborhood of
Y
in
M
such that he
projection map
\text{pr}_{Y}:U_{1}\to Y
is well defined. Let
N>0

and define

\omega_{U}=\omega_{0}+i\partial\bar{\partial}\left(\text{pr}_{Y}^{*}\varphi+Nd_{\rho}^{2}(\cdot,Y)\right).

Clearly, there exists
N>0
and a neighborhood
U_{2}\subset\subset U_{1}

of
Y
such that
\omega_{U}
is
\rm{K\ddot{a}hler}
in
U_{2}
. At a point

p\in Y
, we choose a local normal coordinate
\{z^{i}\}
with respect
to
\rho
such that
\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\cdots,\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{d}}

are tangential to
Y
at
p
,
\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{d+1}},\cdots,\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{n}}

are orthogonal to
Y
, and
\omega_{U}=A_{i\bar{j}}\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2}dz^{i}\wedge d\bar{z}^{j}

with

A=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}H&C\\
C^{\dagger}&V\end{array}\right).

At
p
,
H_{i\bar{j}}\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2}dz^{i}\wedge d\bar{z}^{j}=\pi^{*}\omega_{Y}
,

V_{i\bar{j}}\geq N\delta_{i\bar{j}}
, and
C=0
. In a neighborhood
of
p
, we have
C=O(d_{\rho})
and
V>\frac{N}{2}\text{Id}_{(n-d)\times(n-d)}
.
By the continuity of
\mathcal{P}_{\Lambda}
in Lemma
5.10
,
we only need to verify the cone condition (
9.1
) at

p
. Notice that at
p
, if
N\to\infty
,
A^{-1}
converges to

H^{-1}
uniformly. By (
9.2
), there exists
\epsilon_{Y}>0

such that for any linear subspace
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{T}_{p}Y
,
it holds

1-\epsilon_{Y}>\langle\Lambda,\exp\chi_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}\rangle,

where
\chi_{\mathcal{H}}=\left(H|_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}\right)^{\bar{j}i}2\sqrt{-1}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}^{j}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{i}}
.
Therefore, there exists
N>>1
s.t.
\mathcal{P}_{\Lambda}(A)<1-\frac{\epsilon_{Y}}{2}
.
Hence, by the continuity of
\mathcal{P}_{\Lambda}
and the compactness
of
Y
, we may pick a uniform
N
, a uniform
\epsilon_{Y}
, and
a neighborhood
U\subset\subset U_{2}
of
Y
s.t.
\mathcal{P}_{\Lambda}(\omega_{U})<1-\frac{\epsilon_{Y}}{4}

in
U
, which verifies the cone condition (
9.1
).
∎

We prove Theorem
9.1
by induction
on the dimension of
Y
. We start with the base case.

9.1.
Base case for induction

Since we have assumed the
H1
condition,
\mathring{\Lambda}

is
k_{0}
-UP. Clearly, for any smooth subvariety
Y
of dimension

d>k_{0}
,
\mathring{\Lambda}|_{Y}
is also
k_{0}
-UP. To initiate
the induction argument, we need to show that Theorem
9.1

is valid for any subvareity
Y
with dimension
d\leq k_{0}
. The
following lemma generalizes Song’s Lemma 2.1 in
[
32
]
.

Lemma 9.4
.

Notations as above. Suppose that
\mathring{\Lambda}

is
k_{0}
-UP. Let
Y
be an analytic subvariety of
M
with
\dim Y\leq k_{0}
.
If
[\omega_{0}]
is
(\kappa,[\Lambda])
-positive, then there exists
a neighborhood
U_{Y}
of
Y
in
M
and a Kähler metric
\omega_{U_{Y}}\in[\omega_{0}]|_{U_{Y}}

such that the cone condition (
1.5
) holds for

\omega_{U_{Y}}
in
U_{Y}
.

Proof.

Case 1.
\dim Y=0
, the result is obvious since any Kähler class
in a neighborhood of a point is trivial.

We may assume fron now on that there exists
d\geq 0
such that Lemma

9.4
holds for any
d^{\prime}
dimensional subvariety
Y^{\prime}\subset M

where
d^{\prime}\leq d
. Let
Y
be an subvariety of dimenison
d\leq k_{0}
.

Case 2.
1\leq d\leq k_{0}.
Let
\Lambda^{\prime}=\Lambda^{[d]}
if
k_{0}=d

or
\Lambda^{\prime}=0
if
k_{0}>d
.

Note that
([\Lambda],\kappa)
-positivity implies the following

\int_{Y}(1-\Lambda^{\prime})\wedge\exp\omega>0.

Let
S_{Y}
be the singular set of
Y
. Let
\Phi:M^{\prime}\to M
be
the resolution of singularities of
Y
by successive blowups along
smooth centers. Let
\hat{Y}
be the strict transform of
Y
. Denote
the exceptional divisor
E_{\Phi}
. Let
\sigma
be a defining section
of the line bundle
[E_{\Phi}]
and
h
be a hermitian metric on

[E_{\Phi}]
. Let
F_{h}
be the curvature form on
h
. Then there
is a small
\delta>0
such that
\varpi_{Y}:=\omega_{Y}-\delta F_{h}

is a
\rm{K\ddot{a}hler}
metric on
\Phi^{-1}(W)
. Pick a small
\epsilon
such
that

(9.3)

\int_{Y}\exp\omega-(1+2\epsilon)\Lambda^{\prime}>0.

By choosing a smaller
\delta
if necessary, we may assume that

(9.4)

\int_{\hat{Y}}\exp\varpi-(1+\epsilon)\Phi^{*}\Lambda^{\prime}>0.

On
\hat{Y}
, we may solve the following Monge-Ampèreequation

(9.5)

(\varpi_{Y}+\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}u)^{d}=(1+\epsilon)\Phi^{*}\Lambda^{\prime}+c\varpi_{Y}^{d},

for some constant
c>0
. In
M^{\prime}\backslash E_{\Phi}
, we may write

-F_{h}=\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}\log|\sigma|_{h}^{2}
. Thus, there exists

\varphi_{Y}\in C^{\infty}(W\backslash S_{Y})\cap\text{PSH}(W,\omega_{0})

such that

(\omega_{0}+\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}\varphi_{Y})^{d}-(1+\epsilon)\Lambda^{\prime}>0,

as a
(d,d)
form away from
S_{Y}
; Furthermore, the Lelong number
of
\varphi_{Y}
at
S_{Y}
is larger than
\delta
.

By the induction hypothesis, there exists a neighborhood
U
of
S_{Y}

in which there is a smooth
\rm{K\ddot{a}hler}
metric
\omega_{U}=\omega_{0}+\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}\varphi_{U}

satisfying the condition
((1-\Lambda)\wedge\exp\omega_{U})^{[n-1]}>0
.
We pick neighborhoods
U_{0}\Subset U_{1}\Subset U_{2}\Subset U

of
p
.

Figure 9.1.
Neighborhoods

Without loss of generality, we may subtract a large number from
\varphi_{U}

such that
\varphi_{U}<\varphi_{Y}-2
in
W\backslash U_{2}
. Since

\varphi_{Y}
diverges to
-\infty
at
p
, shrinking
U_{1}

if necessary, we may assume that in
U_{1}
,
\varphi_{Y}+2<\varphi_{U}

in
U_{1}
.

For a point
z\in W\backslash U_{0}
, let
d_{\rho}(z)
be the distant
function to
Y\cap(W\backslash U_{0})
with respect to
\rho
. Let

(9.6)

\tilde{\varphi}_{Y}=\varphi_{Y}+Nd_{\rho}^{2}.

By the same argument as in Lemma
9.3
,
for sufficiently large
N
,
\tilde{\omega}=\omega_{0}+\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}\tilde{\varphi}_{Y}

is a
\rm{K\ddot{a}hler}
form in a neighborhood
\tilde{U}
of
Y\cap(W\backslash U_{1})

and satisfies the cone condition (
9.1
) on
\tilde{U}.

Shrinking
\tilde{U}
if necessary, we may assume that in
\tilde{U}\cap U_{1}

the follow holds:

(9.7)

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}_{Y}

\displaystyle<\varphi_{U}-1,\ \text{in}\ \tilde{U}\cap U_{1};\ \tilde{\varphi}_{Y}>\varphi_{U}+1,\ \text{in }\tilde{U}\backslash U_{2}.

Let

\tilde{\varphi}=\widetilde{\max}_{(1/2,1/2)}(\tilde{\varphi}_{Y},\varphi_{U})

in
U_{Y}=\tilde{U}\cup U_{1}
. By the above construction, we have
on
U_{1}\cap\tilde{U}

\varphi_{U}-\frac{1}{2}>\tilde{\varphi}_{Y}+\frac{1}{2}

and on
\tilde{U}\backslash U_{2}
,
\tilde{\varphi}_{Y}-\frac{1}{2}>\varphi_{U}+\frac{1}{2}
.
Therefore, by Richberg’s technique (Corollary
8.4
),

\tilde{\varphi}
is smooth and in
\text{PSH}(U_{Y},\omega_{0})
.
Moreover, by Corollary
8.3
,
\widetilde{\max}

preserves the subsolution. Thus,
\omega_{0}+\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}\tilde{\varphi}

satisfies the cone condition.
∎

Now that we have established the base case for the induction argument,
from now on, we may assume
\dim Y>k_{0}
in the later discussions.
We state our induction hypothesis.

Induction Hypothesis
: There exists
d\in\mathbb{N}
and
d>k_{0}

such that for any subvariety
Y
if
\dim Y\leq d-1\leq n-1
, then
there exists a neighborhood
U
of
Y
in
M
and a
\rm{K\ddot{a}hler}
metric

\omega_{U}\in[\omega_{0}|_{U}]
such that
\omega_{U}|_{Y}=\omega_{Y}

and
\omega_{U}
satisfies the cone condition (
9.1
)
in
U
.

9.2.
Resolution of singularities

Let
Y
be a subvariety of
M
with
\dim Y=d>k_{0}
. Assume that

Y
is irreducible for simplicity. Apply the resolution of singularities
for
Y
to get

(9.8)

\Phi:M^{\prime}\to M,

where
\Phi
is achieved by a sequence of blow-ups along smooth centers.
Let
\hat{Y}
be the strict transform of
Y
in
M^{\prime}
which is a
smooth
d
-dimensional submanifold. Let
S_{Y}
be the singular
set of
Y
and

(9.9)

S_{\hat{Y}}=\Phi^{-1}(S_{Y})\cap\hat{Y}.

Since
Y
is irreducible,
Y\backslash S_{Y}=\Phi(\hat{Y}\backslash S_{\hat{Y}})
.

Let
S
be the exceptional locus of
\Phi
,
h_{S}
be a hermitian
metric on the line bundle
[S]
associated to
S
, and
F_{h_{S}}

be the curvature. Then for some small
\delta_{S}>0
,
\varpi:=\rho-\delta_{S}F_{h_{S}}

is
\rm{K\ddot{a}hler}
on
M^{\prime}
for some small
\delta_{S}>0
. We may further
assume that

(9.10)

\varpi=\rho-\delta_{S}F_{h_{S}}>\frac{\rho}{2}.

If
\sigma_{S}
is a defining section of the line bundle
[S]
,
and
\phi_{S}=\delta_{S}\log|\sigma_{S}|_{h_{S}}^{2}
then on
M^{\prime}\backslash S

we have

\varpi=\rho+\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}\phi_{S}.

Notice
\phi_{S}
has positive Lelong number along
S
.

We need to perturb
[\omega_{0}]
and
[\Lambda]
to obtain strict

(\kappa,[\Lambda])
-positivity on
\hat{Y}
. For this purpose,
we define the following:

(9.11)

\hat{\omega}_{0}=\hat{\omega}_{0}(t,\hat{\epsilon})=\left(1+Kt\right)\omega_{0}+\hat{\epsilon}t\varpi,\ \hat{\Lambda}=\hat{\Lambda}(t,\hat{\epsilon})=\Lambda+\hat{\epsilon}^{n}t^{n}\frac{\varpi^{k_{0}}}{k_{0}!},

(9.12)

\hat{\rho}=\hat{\rho}(t,\hat{\epsilon})=\rho+\hat{\epsilon}^{n}t^{n}\varpi.

Here
K>1
is chosen large enough such that

(9.13)

\Lambda\leq K\left(\exp(\frac{\omega_{0}}{2n})-1\right).

The following result shows that
[\hat{\omega}_{0}]
is
(\kappa,[\hat{\Lambda}])
-positive.

Lemma 9.5

(Song Lemma 4.1)
.

There is a small
\hat{\epsilon}_{Y}
s.t. if
\hat{\epsilon}\in(0,\hat{\epsilon}_{Y}),\ t\in(0,1]

then

(9.14)

\int_{\hat{Y}}e^{\hat{\omega}_{0}}\wedge\left(1-\hat{\Lambda}\right)>0,

and for any subvariety
V^{\prime}
of
\hat{Y}
with dimension
k<d
,
it holds

(9.15)

\int_{V^{\prime}}e^{\hat{\omega}_{0}}\wedge\left(1-\hat{\Lambda}\right)>\frac{1}{2}\int_{V^{\prime}}\exp(\hat{\epsilon}t\varpi).

Proof.

For
\hat{Y}
, we have

(9.16)

\displaystyle\int_{\hat{Y}}e^{\hat{\omega}_{0}}\wedge\left(1-\hat{\Lambda}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{\hat{Y}}e^{(1+Kt)\omega_{0}+\hat{\epsilon}t\varpi}\wedge(1-\Lambda)-\hat{\epsilon}^{n}t^{n}\int_{\hat{Y}}e^{\hat{\omega}_{0}}\wedge\frac{\varpi^{k_{0}}}{k_{0}!}

\displaystyle\geq(1+Kt)^{d}\int_{Y}e^{\omega_{0}}\wedge(1-\Lambda)+O(\hat{\epsilon}(1+t)^{d}).

Since
\int_{Y}e^{\omega_{0}}\wedge(1-\Lambda)>0
, there is a
\hat{\epsilon}_{Y}^{\prime}>0

so that if
\hat{\epsilon}<\hat{\epsilon}_{Y}^{\prime}
then (
9.14
)
holds.

If
k<d
, we may assume
V^{\prime}
is a irreducible. Let
W=\Phi(V^{\prime})
.
By induction hypothesis, there is a
\rm{K\ddot{a}hler}
form
\omega_{W}
in
a neighborhood
U_{W}
of
W
in
M
so that
\omega_{W}\in[\omega_{0}]|_{W}

and

\left(\exp\omega_{W}\wedge\left(1-\Lambda\right)\right)^{[n-1]}>0.

Then by Lemma
5.8
, for
any
l\leq n-1
,

(\exp\omega_{W}\wedge(1-\Lambda))^{[l]}\geq 0,

which implies

(9.17)

(\exp\omega_{W}\wedge\left(1-\Lambda\right))^{[l]}\wedge\varpi^{k-l}\geq 0,

in
U^{\prime}_{W}=\Phi^{-1}(W)
in
M^{\prime}
for
l\leq k
. Let

(9.18)

\hat{\omega}_{1}:=(1+Kt)\omega_{W}+\hat{\epsilon}t\varpi.

Then by (
9.17
),

(9.19)

\displaystyle\int_{V^{\prime}}e^{\hat{\omega}_{1}}\wedge\left(1-\hat{\Lambda}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{V^{\prime}}e^{(1+Kt)\omega_{W}}\wedge e^{\hat{\epsilon}t\varpi}\wedge(1-\Lambda-\hat{\epsilon}^{n}t^{n}\frac{\varpi^{k_{0}}}{k_{0}!})

\displaystyle=\int_{V^{\prime}}e^{(1+Kt)\omega_{W}}\wedge e^{\hat{\epsilon}t\varpi}\wedge(1-\Lambda)-\hat{\epsilon}^{n}t^{n}\int_{V^{\prime}}e^{(1+Kt)\omega_{W}}\wedge e^{\hat{\epsilon}t\varpi}\wedge\frac{\varpi^{k_{0}}}{k_{0}!}

\displaystyle\geq\int_{V^{\prime}}\frac{(\hat{\epsilon}t)^{k}}{k!}\varpi^{k}-\sum_{a=0}^{k-k_{0}}R(a)\int_{V^{\prime}}\frac{\omega_{W}^{a}}{a!}\wedge\frac{\varpi^{k-a}}{(k-k_{0}-a)!k_{0}!},

where
R(a)=(1+Kt)^{a}(\hat{\epsilon}t)^{n+k-k_{0}-a}
. There is
a uniform constant
C_{1}
, s.t.
C_{1}[\varpi]\geq[\Phi^{*}\omega_{0}]
.
Thus,

(9.20)

\int_{V^{\prime}}e^{\hat{\omega}_{1}}\wedge\left(1-\hat{\Lambda}\right)\geq\left(\frac{(\hat{\epsilon}t)^{k}}{k!}-C_{2}\sum_{a=0}^{k-k_{0}}R(a)\right)\int_{V^{\prime}}\varpi^{k}.

where
C_{2}=C_{2}(C_{1},k,k_{0})
. If
k<k_{0}
, we have

(9.21)

\int_{V^{\prime}}e^{\hat{\omega}_{1}}\wedge\left(1-\hat{\Lambda}\right)>\frac{(\hat{\epsilon}t)^{k}}{k!}\int_{V^{\prime}}\varpi^{k}.

If
k\geq k_{0}
, the power of
\hat{\epsilon}
in
R(a)
is greater
than
k
and the power of
t
is bigger than
n
. Hence, there
is an
\hat{\epsilon}_{Y}^{\prime\prime}=\hat{\epsilon}_{Y}^{\prime\prime}(k,n,C_{2},K)
such
that if
\hat{\epsilon}<\hat{\epsilon}_{Y}^{\prime\prime}
,

(9.22)

R(a)<\frac{1}{2kC_{2}}\frac{(\hat{\epsilon}t)^{k}}{k!}.

Substituting (
9.22
) into (
9.20
)
yields (
9.15
). Finally, choose
\hat{\epsilon}_{Y}=\min(\hat{\epsilon}_{Y}^{\prime},\hat{\epsilon}_{Y}^{\prime\prime})

and we have finished the proof.
∎

We proceed to check the
k_{0}
-UP condition for
\hat{\Lambda}
.

Lemma 9.6
.

If
\mathring{\Lambda}
is
k_{0}
-UP
with respect to
\rho
and a uniform constant
m>0
. Then
\mathring{\hat{\Lambda}}

is
k_{0}
-UP with respect to
\hat{\rho}
and a constant

m^{\prime}=\min\{m,k_{0}\left(\frac{1}{2}+(\hat{\epsilon}t)^{n}\right)^{k_{0}-1}\}.

Proof.

By
9.10
, we have assumed
\varpi>\frac{\rho}{2}
.
Thus

(9.23)

\displaystyle\frac{\hat{\rho}^{k_{0}}}{k_{0}!}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\rho+\hat{\epsilon}^{n}t^{n}\varpi)^{k_{0}}}{k_{0}!}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\rho^{k_{0}}}{k_{0}!}+(\hat{\epsilon}t)^{n}\frac{\varpi^{k_{0}}}{k_{0}!}\left(\sum_{a=0}^{k_{0}-1}\frac{(\hat{\epsilon}t)^{n(k_{0}-1-a)}k_{0}!}{2^{a}a!(k_{0}-a)!}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\rho^{k_{0}}}{k_{0}!}+(\hat{\epsilon}t)^{n}k_{0}\left(\frac{1}{2}+(\hat{\epsilon}t)^{n}\right)^{k_{0}-1}\frac{\varpi^{k_{0}}}{k_{0}!}.

Since
\mathring{\Lambda}\geq m\frac{\rho^{k_{0}}}{k_{0}!}
, by (
9.23
),

\displaystyle\hat{\Lambda}-\hat{\Lambda}^{[n]}

\displaystyle=\mathring{\Lambda}+(\hat{\epsilon}t)^{n}\frac{\varpi^{k_{0}}}{k_{0}!}\geq m\frac{\rho^{k_{0}}}{k_{0}!}+(\hat{\epsilon}t)^{n}\frac{\varpi^{k_{0}}}{k_{0}!}

\displaystyle\geq m^{\prime}\frac{\hat{\rho}^{k_{0}}}{k_{0}!}.

We have proved the Lemma.
∎

9.3.
Equations on
\hat{Y}

Next, we consider the following equation on
\hat{Y}:

(9.24)

(\exp\hat{\omega}\wedge(1-\hat{\Lambda}))^{[d]}=c_{t,\hat{\epsilon}}(\exp\hat{\rho})^{[d]},

for
t>0
,
\hat{\epsilon}\in(0,\hat{\epsilon}_{Y})
, and
\hat{\omega}\in[\hat{\omega}_{0}]
.
Here
c_{t,\hat{\epsilon}}
is a normalization constant defined s.t.

\int_{\hat{Y}}(\exp\hat{\omega}\wedge(1-\hat{\Lambda}))^{[d]}=\int_{\hat{Y}}c_{t,\hat{\epsilon}}(\exp\hat{\rho})^{[d]}.

We may choose a smaller
\hat{\epsilon}_{Y}
if necessary, such that

\hat{\epsilon}_{Y}<\left(\frac{1}{4n!}\right)^{\frac{1}{n-k_{0}}}
.

The following lemma implies that
\hat{\omega}_{0}
is a subsolution
of (
9.24
) for
t=1
.

Lemma 9.7
.

If
K>1
is chosen as in (
9.13
),
we have

(9.25)

e^{(1+K)\omega_{0}}\wedge(1-\Lambda)\geq\frac{1}{2}e^{(1+K)\omega_{0}}

on
M
. Moreover, on
\hat{Y}
, for
t=1
and
\hat{\epsilon}<\left(\frac{1}{4n!}\right)^{\frac{1}{n-k_{0}}}
,
we have

(9.26)

\left(e^{\hat{\omega}_{0}}\wedge(1-\hat{\Lambda})\right)^{[d-1]}>0.

Proof.

By our choice of
K
in (
9.13
),

(9.27)

\displaystyle e^{(1+K)\omega_{0}}(1-\Lambda)

\displaystyle\geq e^{(1+K)\omega_{0}}\wedge((1+K)-Ke^{\frac{\omega_{0}}{2n}})

\displaystyle=\left((1+K)e^{(1+K)\omega_{0}}-Ke^{(1+K+\frac{1}{2n})\omega_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\left((1+K)^{k+1}-K(1+K+\frac{1}{2n})^{k}\right)\frac{\omega_{0}^{k}}{k!},

on
M
. If
k=0
, then
(1+K)-K=1
. If
k\geq 1
then

(9.28)

\displaystyle(1+K)^{k+1}-K(1+K)^{k}\left(1+\frac{1}{2n(1+K)}\right)^{k}\geq(1+K)^{k}K\left(1+\frac{1}{2K}-e^{\frac{1}{2(1+K)}}+\frac{1}{2K}\right).

If
K>1
, we have
1+\frac{1}{2K}>e^{\frac{1}{2(1+K)}}
. By (
9.27
)
and (
9.28
), we have

(9.29)

e^{(1+K)\omega_{0}}\wedge(1-\Lambda)\geq\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{(1+K)^{k}K}{2K}\frac{\omega_{0}^{k}}{k!}=\frac{1}{2}e^{(1+K)\omega_{0}}.

Thus we have proved (
9.25
).

At
t=1
, by (
9.29
), we have

\displaystyle(\exp\hat{\omega}_{0}\wedge(1-\hat{\Lambda}))^{[d-1]}

\displaystyle=\left(e^{(1+K)\omega_{0}}\wedge(1-\Lambda-\hat{\epsilon}^{n}\frac{\varpi^{k_{0}}}{k_{0}!})\wedge e^{\hat{\epsilon}\varpi}\right)^{[d-1]}

\displaystyle\geq\left(e^{(1+K)\omega_{0}}\wedge\left(\frac{1}{2}e^{\hat{\epsilon}\varpi}-\hat{\epsilon}^{n}\frac{\varpi^{k_{0}}}{k_{0}!}\wedge e^{\hat{\epsilon}\varpi}\right)\right)^{[d-1]}

\displaystyle=\left(e^{(1+K)\omega_{0}}\wedge\left(\sum_{k=0}^{d-2}\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{\hat{\epsilon}^{k}}{k!}\varpi^{k}-\sum_{k\geq k_{0}}^{d-2}\frac{\hat{\epsilon}^{n+k-k_{0}}}{(k-k_{0})!k_{0}!}\varpi^{k}\right)\right)^{[d-1]}

\displaystyle\ +\left(\frac{1}{2}\frac{\hat{\epsilon}^{d-1}}{(d-1)!}-\frac{\hat{\epsilon}^{n+d-1-k_{0}}}{k_{0}!(d-1-k_{0})!}\right)\varpi^{d-1}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{4}\frac{\hat{\epsilon}^{d-1}}{(d-1)!}\varpi^{d-1},

since
\hat{\epsilon}<\left(\frac{1}{4n!}\right)^{\frac{1}{n-k_{0}}}
.
∎

We run a continuity argument for (
9.24
). Let

(9.30)

\mathbf{I}_{\hat{\epsilon}}:=\{t\in(0,1]:(\ref{eq:equation on hat Y})\text{ has a smooth solution for }\hat{\epsilon}\in(0,\hat{\epsilon}_{Y})\}.

By Lemma
9.7
,
\hat{\omega}_{0}
is a subsolution
if
t=1
and
\hat{\epsilon}<\hat{\epsilon}_{Y}
. We conclude that

1\in\mathbf{I}_{\hat{\epsilon}}
.
\mathbf{I}_{\hat{\epsilon}}

is clearly open as the cone condition is an open condition. Let

t_{\hat{\epsilon}}:=\inf\mathbf{I}_{\hat{\epsilon}}.

Remark 9.8
.

We may and will assume that
t_{\hat{\epsilon}}=0

without loss of generality. If
t_{\hat{\epsilon}}=t^{\prime}_{\hat{\epsilon}}>0
,
we may replace
\omega_{0}
by
\hat{\omega}_{0}(t_{\hat{\epsilon}}^{\prime},\hat{\epsilon})
.
If
t_{\hat{\epsilon}}^{\prime}>0
,
\hat{\omega}(t_{\hat{\epsilon}}^{\prime},\hat{\epsilon})

is
\rm{K\ddot{a}hler}
. We can go through the same induction argument to prove
Theorem
9.1
. Then (
9.24
)
admits a solution in
[\hat{\omega}_{0}(t_{\hat{\epsilon}}^{\prime},\hat{\epsilon})]
.
By the openness of the cone condition, we have
t_{\hat{\epsilon}}<t_{\hat{\epsilon}}^{\prime}
,
which a contradiction. Therefore, from now on, we assume that
t_{\hat{\epsilon}}=0
.

10.
Mass concentration

In this Section, we prove a mass concentration result for our PDE
based on techniques from
[
14
,
4
,
32
]
.
We use notations as in previous sections.

By (
9.30
), for
t\in\mathbf{I}_{\hat{\epsilon}}
, there
exists a
\omega_{t}\in[\hat{\omega}_{0}(t,\hat{\epsilon})]
solving

(10.1)

\left(\exp\omega_{t}\wedge\left(1-\hat{\Lambda}\right)-c_{t,\hat{\epsilon}}\exp\hat{\rho}\right)^{[d]}=0,

for some
c_{t,\hat{\epsilon}}>0
. Here
\hat{\omega}_{0}
,
\hat{\Lambda}
,

\hat{\rho}
are defined in (
9.11
) and (
9.12
).
As before, we denote
\Omega_{t}=\exp\omega_{t}.

The main result of this section is the following theorem.

Theorem 10.1
.

Under the same assumption as in Theorem
9.1
,
then there exists
\delta>0
, a finite Euclidean ball partition
\mathscr{P}=\{B_{j,3R}\}_{j\in\mathcal{J}}

of
\hat{Y}
,
\varepsilon>0
,
r_{0}>0
, and a Kähler current

\Upsilon\in(1-\delta)[\omega_{0}]
s.t. for all
0<r<r_{0}
,
j\in\mathcal{J}
,

\mathcal{P}_{\Lambda}(\Upsilon^{(r)})<1-\varepsilon,\ \text{in }B_{j,R},

where
\Upsilon^{(r)}
is given in Definition
8.8
.
Furthermore,
\Upsilon
has positive Lelong numbers along
S_{\hat{Y}}.

Remark 10.2
.

We may simplify our argument by making following
assumptions without loss of generality:

(1)

d>k_{0}
, by Lemma
9.4
;

(2)

\mathbf{I}_{\hat{\epsilon}}=(0,1]
, which may be achieved by arguments
in Remark
9.8
;

(3)

\hat{\Lambda}^{[d]}=0
; Since the subsolution condition is only
related to the components of degree less than
d
; If
\hat{\Lambda}^{[d]}\not=0
,
we may take another
c_{t,\hat{\epsilon}}^{\prime}\geq 0
such that

\int_{\hat{Y}}\exp\omega_{t}\wedge(1-\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}\hat{\Lambda}^{[k]})=c^{\prime}_{t,\hat{\epsilon}}\int_{\hat{Y}}\exp\hat{\rho};

Therefore, after replacing
\hat{\Lambda}
by
\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}\hat{\Lambda}^{[k]}

and
c_{t,\hat{\epsilon}}
by
c^{\prime}_{t,\hat{\epsilon}}
, the cone
condition does not change and the solvability is not affected;

(4)

\hat{\Lambda}
is
k_{0}
-UP with respect to
\hat{\rho}
and
a uniform constant
m^{\prime}>0
. This reduction is possible due to Lemma

9.6
.

10.1.
The lifted equation on the product manifold

Following
[
4
]
, we consider a new equation on the product
space
\mathcal{Y}=\hat{Y}\times\hat{Y}
. We fix some notations:
On
\mathcal{Y}
, let
\pi_{1}:\mathcal{Y}\to\hat{Y}
,
(x,y)\mapsto x

and
\pi_{2}:\mathcal{Y}\to\hat{Y},\ (x,y)\mapsto y
be canonical
projections. Let
\hat{Y}_{1}=\pi_{1}(\mathcal{Y})
,
\hat{Y}_{2}=\pi_{2}(\mathcal{Y})
,
and let
\iota_{i}:\hat{Y}_{i}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Y}
be canonical
embeddings. We denote

(10.2)

\Lambda_{x}:=\pi_{1}^{*}\hat{\Lambda},\ \Lambda_{y}:=\frac{1}{d}\pi_{2}^{*}\hat{\rho}.

We make the following observation:
\hat{\rho}
can be viewed as
a solution to the equation of
\omega\in[\hat{\rho}]:

(\frac{1}{d}\hat{\rho}\wedge\exp\omega)^{[d]}=\frac{\omega^{d}}{d!}

because of the simple fact

(10.3)

\left(\frac{1}{d}\hat{\rho}\wedge\exp\hat{\rho}\right)^{[d]}=\frac{\hat{\rho}^{d}}{d!}.

Let

(10.4)

\boldsymbol{\Lambda}:=\Lambda_{x}+\Lambda_{y},\ \boldsymbol{\rho}:=\pi_{1}^{*}\hat{\rho}+\pi_{2}^{*}\hat{\rho},\ \boldsymbol{\varpi}=\pi_{1}^{*}\varpi+\pi_{2}^{*}\varpi.

Let
\{B_{j}\}
be a finite open cover of
\Delta=\{(x,x):x\in\hat{Y}\}\subset\hat{Y}\times\hat{Y}

with balls
B_{j}
. Let
\theta_{j}^{2}
be a partition of unity
subordinate to
B_{j}
. Let
g_{j,k}
be the defining function of

\Delta
in
B_{j}
. Let

(10.5)

\psi=\frac{1}{2}\log(\sum_{j,k}\theta_{j}^{2}|g_{j,k}|^{2}),\ \psi_{s}=\frac{1}{2}\log(\sum_{j,k}\theta_{j}^{2}|g_{j,k}|^{2}+s^{2}).

Define

(10.6)

\boldsymbol{\rho}_{s}=\boldsymbol{\rho}+\delta_{\rho}\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}\left(\pi_{1}^{*}\phi_{S}+\pi_{2}^{*}\phi_{S}\right)+\delta_{\rho}^{2}\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}\psi_{s}.

\delta_{\rho}
is chosen small but fixed so that
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{s}

is still
\rm{K\ddot{a}hler}
. We will determined
\delta_{\rho}
later in Proposition

10.4
. Let

(10.7)

f_{t,s}=\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{\rho}_{s}^{2d}}{\boldsymbol{\rho}^{2d}}-(1+c_{t,\hat{\epsilon},\delta_{\rho}})\right)+c_{t,\hat{\epsilon}}.

where
c_{t,\hat{\epsilon},\delta_{\rho}}
is chosen such that

\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\left(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{s}^{2d}-(1+c_{t,\hat{\epsilon},\delta_{\rho}})\boldsymbol{\rho}^{2d}\right)=0.

Note,
c_{t,\hat{\epsilon},\delta_{\rho}}
is uniformly bounded for
any
t\in(0,1)
and
\hat{\epsilon}\in(0,\hat{\epsilon}_{Y})
.

We denote
\boldsymbol{\tau}=(t,s)
with
t,s>0
. We consider

the following lifted equation on
\mathcal{Y}
:

(10.8)

2\frac{\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{2d}}{(2d)!}=\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{[k]}\wedge\frac{\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{2d-k}}{(2d-k)!}+f_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\frac{\boldsymbol{\rho}^{2d}}{(2d)!},

for
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\in[\pi_{1}^{*}\omega_{t}+\pi_{2}^{*}\hat{\rho}]
.
If we denote

(10.9)

\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}:=\exp\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\boldsymbol{\tau}},\ \mathbf{P}:=\exp\boldsymbol{\rho},

then we may re-write (
10.8
) as

(10.10)

2\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{[2d]}=(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\wedge\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\boldsymbol{\tau}})^{[2d]}+f_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\mathbf{P}^{[2d]}.

Lemma 10.3
.

Notations as above. The canonical
splitting gives a labeled splitting
\mathcal{O}
with respect to

\boldsymbol{\rho}
. Moreover,
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
is
\mathcal{O}
-UP.

Proof.

Following arguments in Example
2.8
, the product structure
gives the labeled splitting

\mathcal{O}_{(x,y)}=\{2,(d,d),\{\mathcal{T}_{x}\hat{Y}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{y}\hat{Y}_{2}\},(k_{0},1)\}.

By Lemma
9.6
, we have

\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\geq\min\{m^{\prime},\frac{1}{d}\}\left(\frac{\hat{\rho}^{k_{0}}(x)}{k_{0}!}+\hat{\rho}(y)\right).

Hence,
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
is
\mathcal{O}
-UP with uniform constant

m^{\prime\prime}=\min\{m^{\prime},\frac{1}{d}\}
.
∎

Proposition 10.4
.

Notations as above. For

\delta_{\rho}
small, there is a smooth solution to (
10.8
)
if
\Lambda
satisfies condition
H1.

Proof.

By Lemma
10.3
,
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}

satisfies
\mathcal{O}
-UP condition with a uniform constant
m^{\prime\prime}>0
.
Let

f_{\min}=-\min\left\{\frac{m^{\prime\prime}}{8d+2}\gamma_{\min}(\frac{2\kappa}{m^{\prime\prime}},2,(d,d),(k_{0},1)),\frac{\kappa\int_{\mathcal{Y}}(\pi_{1}^{*}\omega_{t}+\pi_{2}^{*}\hat{\rho})^{2d}}{2\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\boldsymbol{\rho}^{2d}}\right\}.

Note
f_{\min}<-c<0
where
c
can be chosen independent of
\hat{\epsilon}

and
t
. From Lemma
10.10
, there exits a uniform small

\delta_{\rho}
independent of
\hat{\epsilon}
and
t
such that

f_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}>f_{\min}
. Therefore,
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}+f_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\mathbf{P}^{[2d]}

satisfies condition
H2
. It is easy to check that integrals
of both sides of (
10.8
) match. Thus apply Theorem

1.7
, one only needs to check the cone condition.

Let

(10.11)

\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}=\pi_{1}^{*}\omega_{t}+\pi_{2}^{*}\rho,\ \mathbf{\Omega}_{\text{0}}=\exp\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0},\ \hat{P}=\exp\hat{\rho}.

We claim
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}\in\mathcal{C}_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{2}
.
Since

(10.12)

\mathbf{\Omega}_{0}^{[2d-1]}=\Omega_{t}(x)^{[d]}\wedge P(y)^{[d-1]}+\Omega_{t}(x)^{[d-1]}\wedge P(y)^{[d]},

we have

(10.13)

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\wedge\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{0})^{[2d-1]}

\displaystyle=\left(\Lambda_{x}\wedge\Omega_{t}(x)\right)^{[d]}\wedge\hat{P}(y)^{[d-1]}+\left(\Lambda_{x}\wedge\Omega_{t}(x)\right)^{[d-1]}\wedge\hat{P}(y)^{[d]}

\displaystyle+\Omega_{t}(x)^{[d-1]}\wedge\left(\Lambda_{y}\wedge\hat{P}(y)\right)^{[d]}+\Omega_{t}(x)^{[d]}\wedge(\Lambda_{y}\wedge\hat{P}(y))^{[d-1]}.

By equation (
10.1
),

(10.14)

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\wedge\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{0})^{[2d-1]}

\displaystyle=\left(\Omega_{t}-c_{t,\hat{\epsilon}}\hat{P}(x)\right)^{[d]}\wedge\hat{P}(y)^{[d-1]}+\left(\Lambda_{x}\wedge\Omega_{t}\right)^{[d-1]}\wedge\hat{P}(y)^{[d]}

\displaystyle+\Omega_{t}{}^{[d-1]}\wedge\hat{P}(y)^{[d]}+\Omega_{t}(x)^{[d]}\wedge(\Lambda_{y}\wedge\hat{P}(y))^{[d-1]}.

Since
\omega_{t}
satisfies the corresponding cone condition of
equation (
10.1
), we have

(10.15)

\left(\Lambda_{x}\wedge\Omega_{t}\right)^{[d-1]}<\Omega_{t}{}^{[d-1]}.

Similarly,
\rho
satisfies equation (
10.3
), which
implies

(10.16)

(\Lambda_{y}\wedge\hat{P}(y))^{[d-1]}<\hat{P}(y)^{[d-1]}.

Combining (
10.15
), (
10.16
), and (
10.14
),
we obtain

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\wedge\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{0})^{[2d-1]}

\displaystyle<\Omega_{t}(x)^{[d]}\wedge\hat{P}(y)^{[d-1]}+\Omega_{t}(x)^{[d-1]}\wedge\hat{P}(y)^{[d]}

\displaystyle+\Omega_{t}(x)^{[d-1]}\wedge\hat{P}(y)^{[d]}+\Omega_{t}(x)^{[d]}\wedge\hat{P}(y)^{[d-1]}

\displaystyle=2\Omega_{t}(x)^{[d]}\wedge\hat{P}(y)^{[d-1]}+2\Omega_{t}(x)^{[d-1]}\wedge\hat{P}(y)^{[d]}

\displaystyle=2\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{0}^{[2d-1]}.

Therefore,
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{2}

satisfies the cone condition. By Theorem
1.7
,
there exists a smooth solution to (
1.7
).
∎

We illustrate the construction of the lifted equation with the following
example.

Example 10.5
.

Suppose that
Y
is smooth of dimension
3 and
\Lambda=\rho^{2}
. Then equations (
10.1
)
and (
10.3
) imply that

(10.17)

\frac{\omega_{t}^{3}}{3!}=\rho^{2}\wedge\omega_{t}+c_{t}\frac{\rho^{3}}{3!},\ \frac{\rho^{3}}{3!}=\frac{\rho}{3}\wedge\frac{\rho^{2}}{2!}.

Let
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}=\pi_{1}^{*}\omega_{t}+\pi_{2}^{*}\rho

and
\boldsymbol{\omega}\in[\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}]
. The lifted
equation on
\mathcal{Y}
is

(10.18)

2\frac{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{6}}{6!}=\pi_{1}^{*}\rho^{2}\wedge\frac{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{4}}{4!}+\frac{1}{3}\pi_{2}^{*}\rho\wedge\frac{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{5}}{5!}+f_{t,s}\frac{(\pi_{1}^{*}\rho+\pi_{2}^{*}\rho)^{6}}{6!}.

We use (
10.17
) and corresponding cone conditions to obtain
that

\displaystyle 2\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}^{5}/5!

\displaystyle=2\frac{\pi_{1}^{*}\omega_{t}^{3}}{3!}\wedge\frac{\pi_{2}^{*}\rho^{2}}{2}+2\frac{\pi_{1}^{*}\omega_{t}^{2}}{2}\wedge\frac{\pi_{2}^{*}\rho^{3}}{3!}

\displaystyle>\pi_{1}^{*}\left(\rho^{2}\wedge\omega_{t}\right)\wedge\frac{\pi_{2}^{*}\rho^{2}}{2}+\pi_{1}^{*}\rho^{2}\wedge\frac{\pi_{2}^{*}\rho^{3}}{3!}

\displaystyle+\frac{\pi_{1}^{*}\omega_{t}^{3}}{3!}\wedge\pi_{2}^{*}\rho\wedge\frac{\pi_{2}^{*}\rho}{3}+\frac{\pi_{1}^{*}\omega_{t}^{2}}{2}\wedge\frac{\pi_{2}^{*}\rho^{2}}{2}\wedge\frac{\pi_{2}^{*}\rho}{3}

\displaystyle=\pi_{1}^{*}\rho^{2}\wedge\frac{\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}^{3}}{3!}+\pi_{2}^{*}\frac{\rho}{3}\wedge\frac{\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}^{4}}{4!}.

Thus
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}
is a subsolution to (
10.18
).

Suppose that
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
is a solution
to the equation (
10.8
). At
(x,y)\in\mathcal{Y}
,
we pick a coordinate
\{x^{i}\}
,
\{y^{i}\}
near
x
and
y
,
respectively. Then,
\boldsymbol{\omega}
is represented by a Hermitian
matrix

\mathbf{A}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}H&D\\
D^{\dagger}&V\end{array}\right).

We write

(10.19)

\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=\boldsymbol{\omega}_{x}+\boldsymbol{\omega}_{y}+\boldsymbol{\omega}_{m}+\bar{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{m},

where

(10.20)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\omega}_{x}

\displaystyle=\pi_{1}^{*}\iota_{1}^{*}\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2}H_{i\bar{j}}dx^{i}\wedge d\bar{x}^{j},

(10.21)

\boldsymbol{\omega}_{y}=\pi_{2}^{*}\iota_{2}^{*}\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2}V_{i\bar{j}}dy^{i}\wedge d\bar{y}^{j},

(10.22)

\boldsymbol{\omega}_{m}=\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2}D_{i\bar{j}}dx^{i}\wedge d\bar{y}^{j}.

Let

(10.23)

\hat{c}=\hat{c}(t,\hat{\epsilon}):=\left[\frac{\hat{\rho}^{d}}{d!}\right]\cdot\hat{Y}.

Define

(10.24)

\displaystyle\omega_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\hat{c}}\int_{\{x\}\times\hat{Y}}\left(\Lambda_{y}\wedge\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\right)^{[d+1]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\hat{c}}\int_{\{x\}\times\hat{Y}}\left(\left(\Lambda_{y}\wedge\exp\boldsymbol{\omega}_{y}\right)^{[d]}\wedge\boldsymbol{\omega}_{x}+\left(\Lambda_{y}\wedge\exp\boldsymbol{\omega}_{y}\right)^{[d-1]}\wedge\boldsymbol{\omega}_{m}\wedge\bar{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{m}\right).

Lemma 10.6
.

Notations as above. We have
\omega_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\in[\hat{\omega}_{0}]
.

Proof.

Direct computation shows

(10.25)

\displaystyle[\omega_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\hat{c}}\left[\int_{\{x\}\times\hat{Y}}\left(\Lambda_{y}\wedge\frac{\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{d}}{d!}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\hat{c}}\left(\frac{1}{d}[\Lambda_{y}]\cdot\frac{[\boldsymbol{\omega}_{y}]^{d-1}}{(d-1)!}\cdot\hat{Y}\right)[\omega_{t}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\hat{c}}\left(\frac{[\hat{\rho}]^{d}}{d!}\cdot\hat{Y}\right)[\hat{\omega}_{0}]

\displaystyle=[\hat{\omega}_{0}].

∎

We list following definitions.

(10.26)

\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\omega},\mathbf{\Lambda}):=\frac{\left(\mathbf{\Lambda}\wedge\exp\boldsymbol{\omega}\right)^{[2d]}}{(\exp\boldsymbol{\omega})^{[2d]}}.

For convenience, we abuse the notation and write

(10.27)

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}\left(\mathbf{A}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\omega},\mathbf{\Lambda}),

and define

(10.28)

F_{1}(H):=\frac{\left(\Lambda_{x}\wedge\exp\boldsymbol{\omega}_{x}\right)^{[d]}}{\left(\exp\boldsymbol{\omega}_{x}\right)^{[d]}},

(10.29)

F_{2}(V):=\frac{\left(\Lambda_{y}\wedge\exp\boldsymbol{\omega}_{y}\right)^{[d]}}{\left(\exp\boldsymbol{\omega}_{y}\right)^{[d]}}.

We also define

(10.30)

\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{\Lambda}}(\mathbf{A})=\max_{\mathbf{B}\in\overline{\Gamma_{2n\times 2n}^{+}},\|\mathbf{B}\|=1}\lim_{t\to+\infty}\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{A}+t\mathbf{B}),

(10.31)

\mathcal{P}_{1}(H)=\mathcal{P}_{\hat{\Lambda}}(H)=\max_{B\in\overline{\Gamma_{n\times n}^{+}},\|B\|=1}\lim_{t\to+\infty}F_{1}(H+tB),

(10.32)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{2}(V)

\displaystyle=\max_{B\in\overline{\Gamma_{n\times n}^{+}},\|B\|=1}\lim_{t\to+\infty}F_{2}(V+tB).

It is easy to check that
\mathbf{F},F_{1},F_{2},\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{\Lambda}},\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2}

are all functions satisfying monotone and convexity conditions by
Lemma
5.10
.

Lemma 10.7
.

Notations as above. We have

(10.33)

\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{A})\geq F_{1}(H-DV^{-1}D^{\dagger})+F_{2}(V).

Moreover,

(10.34)

\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{\Lambda}}(\mathbf{A})\geq\mathcal{P}_{1}(H-DV^{-1}D^{\dagger})+F_{2}(V).

Proof.

We denote

\displaystyle\chi
